# Roostack Specification

This document records decisions (locked and pending) and the reasoning behind them. See PLAN.md for the project brief and TASKS.md for progress.

Status legend: **Locked** = agreed, **Pending** = under discussion, **Open** = not yet discussed.

**Name (2026-09-28):** the project is **Roostack**, pronounced "Roost Stack" (`roostack` in code, packages, paths and the Linux user). Its domain is **roostack.cloud** (the Go module path is `roostack.cloud/roostack`); **roost.ac** is for subdomains Roostack hosts for people's labs (§6.5). Earlier drafts called it MyLab; there was no release, so nothing migrates from that name.

---

## 1. Guiding Principles — Locked

1. **Recoverable by default.** A user can rebuild their entire lab on new hardware from a single backup. This is the main test for design decisions.
2. **Declarative state.** Roostack stores the *desired* state (services, domains, settings) as files; podman is reconciled to match. This simplifies backups, workers, and debugging.
3. **Local-first.** Everything works without Roostack Cloud or internet access, except public TLS issuance and image pulls.
4. **Transparent output.** Generated artifacts (container definitions, proxy config) are human-readable and inspectable. A comfortable homelabber can always find where things live and what Roostack did.
5. **Simplicity.** Small codebase, minimal dependencies, each chosen collaboratively for maintainability.
6. **Secure by default.** Rootless where possible, nothing public unless explicitly opted in.

## 2. Target Users — Locked

- **Primary:** less technical users who manage a lab after someone else has installed it (e.g. a NUC set up by a family member). Day-to-day management, including installing new services, must be possible entirely from the web UI.
- **Secondary:** comfortable homelabbers, who must be able to inspect and understand everything on disk.
- **Rationale:** Installing Linux and Roostack is an acceptable technical barrier; managing the lab afterwards must not be.

## 3. Deployment Targets — Locked

- **v1:** a dedicated, freshly installed server. Roostack assumes it owns the host.
- **Reference hardware:** x86_64 Intel NUC-style device.
- **ARM64 — as built (2026-10-13):** the same package for arm64 (`roostack_<version>_arm64.deb`), for ARM mini PCs and ARM cloud servers running Debian's own Debian 13 (§3.1). Every pinned image must have amd64 and arm64 builds (`make check-images`); a service whose image has none for a server's processor fails with a sentence saying so. Plan and progress: ARM64-PLAN.md.
- **Next:** Raspberry Pi 4 and 5 on Raspberry Pi OS (ARM64-PLAN.md, Phases 2–4). Older Pis (3, Zero 2 W) unsupported for now; reconsidered once Roostack's own memory use is measured (2026-10-13).
- **Rationale:** A dedicated host avoids conflicts with existing software (ports, podman config, firewall) and greatly reduces the support burden.

### 2.1 Licence — Locked (2026-10-18)

- **Apache-2.0** (the project's Owner; assumed from 2026-10-13, confirmed 2026-10-18). Roostack is open source: `LICENSE`, with `NOTICE`, and the package's copyright file says the same.
- **Why:** permissive like MIT, plus an explicit patent grant and a statement that contributions come under the same licence, which suits infrastructure others build on. Roostack Cloud imports Roostack's public packages (`cloudapi`, `authkit`), so a permissive licence lets it use contributors' code without a contributor agreement.
- **Considered:** MIT (the simplest, but no patent clause; the Owner's first thought); AGPL-3.0 (would stop a closed hosted fork, but Cloud's use of contributed code would then need contributors to sign over rights, and it puts some contributors off).
- Packaged services keep their own licences; the website shows each one.

### 3.1 Supported Distros — Locked

| Tier | Distros | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| **Fully supported** | Debian 13 on x86_64; Debian 13 on arm64, from Debian's own installer or cloud image, on a machine that boots it with UEFI | Tested; installer supported; full host OS management (§3.2) |
| **Planned** | Ubuntu 26.04 LTS | Add when practical; avoid Debian-only assumptions that would make it hard |
| **Unsupported** | Anything else with Podman 5+, including ARM boards that need a vendor's image or kernel (e.g. Armbian) | May work; no host OS management, no support |

- **Rationale:** Debian 13 ships Podman 5.4, has a ~5-year support cycle, rarely changes, and has built-in unattended security updates, which makes it well suited to servers nobody logs into. Supporting a single distro keeps the installer, testing and docs simple.
- **Rejected:** Fedora Server (a major upgrade every ~13 months doesn't suit unattended servers); Ubuntu 24.04 (Podman 4.9 has no `.pod` Quadlets); Fedora CoreOS (unfamiliar and harder to troubleshoot).
- Distro-specific logic (package management, update tooling) should sit behind a small internal interface so Ubuntu can be added cleanly.
- **ARM64 (2026-10-13):** a UEFI arm64 machine on Debian 13 runs Debian's own kernel and updates, so to Roostack it's no different from the NUC. Boards that need a vendor's kernel get their kernel and updates from elsewhere, so §3.2 can't promise anything for them. Raspberry Pi OS will be the one vendor OS supported (ARM64-PLAN.md). Considered: Armbian (many boards, each with its own kernel and quirks; later, if people ask).

### 3.2 Host OS Management — Locked

- **Look — Locked (2026-09-27):** a design system with built-in colour themes (docs/DESIGN.md). The default is Teal and violet; each user can choose another (Ocean and magenta, or themes from popular open-source palettes: Catppuccin, Gruvbox, Nord, Solarized, Everforest, Rosé Pine, Tokyo Night) and light, dark or follow the device. Every theme must pass WCAG contrast checks in both modes (a test enforces it).
- **Brand mark and wordmark — Locked (2026-10-01), as built:** chosen with the project's Owner from mock-ups. Files, colours and how to regenerate them: `assets/brand/README.md`.
  - **Marks:** **Perch** (a hen on two rack servers with green "online" lights) is the primary mark; **Nest and egg** (a cracked egg on three woven layers) is the alternate. Each has a Roostack Cloud version (the hen on a cloud server, the egg in a cloud). A simpler **small mark** (the hen on one server) is the favicon, the app's top-bar mark and the help site's logo, because the full mark's details blur below about 32px.
  - **Colours:** the default theme's own: text colour on a `--highlight-soft` tile, the comb, wattle and cloud in `--highlight`, the egg's crack and every other cut-out in the tile colour, and the online light in the status green that shows on a text-coloured shape (`--ok-on-fg`). In Roostack the mark follows the user's theme; the files in `assets/brand` use Teal and violet. Considered: the first draft's own palette (red comb, amber beak, blue cloud: red reads as "danger" in Roostack, and it ignored the themes), a violet bird, and gold or coral accents outside the themes; the Owner preferred the quieter tile.
  - **Wordmark:** lowercase "roostack" in Space Grotesk (OFL): "roo" medium and "stack" bold for Perch, all medium for Nest; Roostack Cloud adds "cloud" in the highlight. The text is converted to outlines, so nothing loads a web font (the UI and help keep the device's own fonts, §6.3.1).
  - **Taglines** (for the website, not the UI): Rule your own roost. · Bring your stack home to roost. · Every service, home to roost. · Hatch your own cloud. · Your whole lab, under one roost. · Free-range servers. Fenced-in security.
- **Feedback on host operations — Locked (2026-09-27):** anything that acts on the host (OS updates, backups, applying service changes, setup hooks, restores) shows a one-line status that updates live ("OS updates: Installing updates…", then "✓ … finished: Installed 3 updates"), with the full step-by-step log and command output under a collapsed **Details** for technical users. Pages refresh their results when the operation finishes. Logs are kept in memory (latest run of each kind); results that matter later are stored separately.

- On fully supported distros, Roostack manages the host OS: security/package updates, reboot handling, and eventually major version upgrades.
- Users must be told clearly (docs and UI) that major manual changes to the host OS may limit Roostack's ability to manage it.
- **Rationale:** The primary user (§2) cannot maintain a Linux host. An unpatched host undermines everything else.

#### Time zone — Locked (2026-09-26)

- Roostack shows and schedules everything (nightly backups, maintenance windows) in the **host's** time zone.
- The setup wizard's first step confirms it, pre-filled from the browser's zone; the Owner can change it in Settings. `roostack-agent` applies it with `timedatectl set-timezone`, then Roostack restarts, because Go reads the local zone once at startup.
- If the browser's UTC offset differs from the host's, every page shows a prominent banner saying times are the server's, with a link for the Owner to change it.
- **Rationale:** cloud images and VMs default to UTC, which silently moved the "02:00" backup to midday for a user in Melbourne.

#### Update & reboot policy — Locked

| What | Policy |
|---|---|
| Security updates | Automatic, daily (`unattended-upgrades`, managed by `roostack-agent`) |
| Other Debian stable updates | Automatic (stable only receives bug and security fixes) |
| Reboots | "Reboot pending" shown in the UI; automatic reboot in a maintenance window (default 04:00). The Owner can change the window or choose notify-only |
| After reboot | Services return through systemd; Roostack health-checks them and flags failures |
| Major upgrades (e.g. 13 → 14) | Never automatic. Owner-initiated, guided, only once Roostack supports the new release, and only after a fresh successful backup |
| Roostack itself | Patch releases automatic; minor/major releases notify and need approval (same as services, §7) |
| Unknown host changes | Basic detection (extra apt sources, held packages, a changed firewall) triggers a warning; more thorough checks later. As built (2026-09-28): per server, on its Updates page and in "needs attention"; the Owner can mark the current changes as made on purpose, and anything new warns again |

- `unattended-upgrades`' own automatic-reboot option is **disabled**; only Roostack decides when to reboot.
- **How it runs (2026-09-27):** Roostack ships `/etc/apt/apt.conf.d/52roostack-unattended-upgrades` (adds stable-updates, no automatic reboot, removes old kernels). Every night after the 02:00 backup, the coordinator runs an "OS updates" job: `roostack-agent` runs `apt-get update && unattended-upgrade` in a transient unit (outside its own sandbox). The job is queued separately from the backup, so a failing backup doesn't hold back security fixes. Debian's own apt timers stay enabled as a fallback. `/run/reboot-required` (written by the kernel's postinst hook) is checked after each run and every 30 minutes; the coordinator then reboots in the window through the agent.
- The Owner sees the last run, any pending restart and why it waits, and can **check for updates now**, **restart now** (refused while a maintenance job runs), and choose automatic restarts at a chosen hour or notify-only (`lab.yaml`: `maintenance.restart`, `maintenance.restart_hour`).
- **Rationale for automatic reboots:** otherwise the primary user may run an unpatched kernel indefinitely. A brief overnight interruption is the better trade-off.

#### Maintenance coordinator — Locked

A single coordinator in `roostack` sequences every disruptive operation (backups, prunes, restores, service updates, package upgrades, reboots) so that conflicting operations never overlap.

- **Nightly order:** backup → automatic service updates → reboot if needed. This means there's always a fresh backup just before any automatic change.
- **Reboots wait** for running backups, restores and updates, then happen once they finish, within the window.
- **Never interrupted:** restores, major OS upgrades, and a backup's prepare step (services stopped or database dumps being written).
- **Resumable long jobs** (e.g. a multi-day first off-site upload) may be paused for a reboot that has been pending for longer than a deadline (default 24h, configurable), and they resume automatically afterwards. restic's repository stays consistent when interrupted.
- The UI explains why a reboot is waiting (e.g. "waiting for backup to finish, 62%").
- **Rationale:** fixed time windows can't handle jobs of unpredictable length. Delaying kernel security fixes for days is worse than pausing a resumable upload.

## 4. Roostack Cloud — Locked (scope), Open (design)

- A future paid hosting service for: resilience (critical services away from home internet), off-site backup targets, and users without their own hardware who still want to own their data.
- **Out of scope for v1**, but worker communication and backup formats must not preclude it.
- Implication: data portability is essential. A user must be able to move services and backups between home, VPS, and Cloud freely.

## 5. Users & Access — Locked

- **Roostack accounts are separate from service accounts in v1.** Roostack manages who can log in to Roostack; each service (Immich, Vaultwarden, …) manages its own users. Unifying them is part of the deferred SSO decision, and the design should allow Roostack to become the identity source later (single sign-on, decided and built 2026-10-20: §5.2).
- **Fixed roles (not customisable in v1):**

| Role | Permissions |
|---|---|
| **Owner** | Everything: host settings, OS updates, backups and restore, recovery key, user management. At least one Owner must exist. |
| **Admin** | Install, configure, update and remove services; run backups; view logs. No host-level settings, no user management. |
| **Member** | View the dashboard (service links, status) only. |

  - Rationale: the person who sets up the lab (Owner) keeps control of risky host settings, while the day-to-day user (Admin) can still manage services freely.
- **Authentication:** local accounts, passwords hashed with argon2id. **TOTP 2FA** in v1, required for Owners and for any public admin UI. Passkeys later.
  - **Who must use two-factor — as built (2026-10-16):** Owners always; Admins while Roostack's own pages are public (a lab on the internet whose UI isn't kept to an SSH tunnel, §6.12), since an Admin can reveal service secrets, run actions and change services. Until they turn it on, every page sends them to Two-factor, which says why (the Owner during first-run setup: the wizard's first step, §6.3). Members never have to (they only see the dashboard). Making the UI public later asks existing Admins to enrol at their next page. Found by the security review of 2026-10-16 (M16): only Owners were asked. Considered, and chosen against by the project's Owner: requiring it of Admins on every lab (simpler, but a home lab's Admins reach the UI only from home).
- **Shared with Roostack Cloud — as built (2026-10-07):** the password, token and two-factor code is a public package, `authkit`, which Cloud's repository imports: one implementation. Both use the same argon2id settings (a lab has far fewer logins than Cloud, so it can always afford at least as much). Cloud, holding many accounts, also turns on a **pepper** (a key outside the database mixed into passwords) and **sealed two-factor secrets** (encrypted with a key outside the database), so a stolen database alone gives neither; a lab's database and keys are on one machine, where they wouldn't help. Fuzz tests try random input against it: the first runs found that a stored hash with out-of-range settings made argon2 panic (now refused, with limits on memory and rounds), and that base64 accepted several spellings of one value (now strict). Considered: two independent implementations, against a bug shared by both (twice the security-sensitive code to keep right).
- **Coming back after logging in — as built (2026-10-21), the Owner's choice:** a page asked for while logged out (a bookmark, `/immich-photos` from the sign-in page's link, a link in an email) sends the login page `?next=<that page>`, and logging in, a two-factor code, and the steps Roostack asks for first (a password of their own, two-factor, an Owner's email address) carry on to it rather than the dashboard. Only a page of Roostack's own: a path on the same address, never another site or the login page (`localNext`); anything else goes to the dashboard. A part of a page refreshing itself comes back to that page (htmx's `HX-Current-URL`). The setup wizard's steps don't carry it: an Owner finishing setup goes on through the wizard. **Considered:** a cookie instead of the parameter (the step is the same, but a parameter shows where you're going and needs no state).
- **Password recovery:** host command line only (`sudo roostack user reset-password <name>`, `reset-2fa <name>` for a lost phone). No email reset (it would need mail setup and adds attack surface), and no recovery codes: the command line is simpler for a home lab.
- **People page (2026-09-27):** the Owner adds people (a temporary password is shown once and must be changed at first login), changes roles, gives someone a new temporary password, turns off someone's two-factor, and removes people. Not on their own account (use Account), and the last Owner can't be demoted or removed. An Owner who forgot their own password uses the command line.
- **Signing out after inactivity — Locked (2026-10-03):** a session ends after a while without activity, 30 minutes by default; the Owner chooses 15 or 30 minutes, 1 or 4 hours, or never (Settings → People → Signing in). Activity is opening a page or sending a form; parts of pages refreshing themselves (htmx requests) aren't, so an open tab doesn't keep a session alive, and once it has ended they take the page to the login page, which says why. Without "keep me signed in" the cookie also ends when the browser closes. **"Keep me signed in"** on the login page (offered unless the Owner turns it off) keeps that browser signed in for 30 days with no idle limit, for someone's own device; it carries through the two-factor step and a password change. Sessions record when they were last used (saved at most every 30 seconds); the hourly session clean-up also deletes those past the idle limit, so relaxing the limit later doesn't bring old ones back. Considered: counting refreshes as activity (a tab left open would never sign out), and a countdown warning before signing out (more code and noise; the page simply goes to the login page).
- **Audit log:** records who did what and when, to support remote help and troubleshooting. *Since 2026-10-16* it also records failed logins (naming the account only when one has that name, so a password typed into the username box isn't kept, in the journal either), a person's change to their own notification address, new public-access check links, cancelling or dismissing Roostack Cloud's link notes, and risks accepted for the Owner's own packages on install and update (security review of 2026-10-16).

### 5.1 Roostack Data Storage — Locked

- **Desired state:** readable YAML files in `/var/lib/roostack/` (Principles 2 and 4).
- **Runtime data** (users, sessions, audit log, job history): a **SQLite** database in `/var/lib/roostack/`. A single file, no server, easy to back up. The backup prepare step must take a consistent copy (SQLite online backup / `VACUUM INTO`), not a raw copy of the file.

### 5.2 Single sign-on for services — Locked (2026-10-20); first version as built (2026-10-20)

One sign-in for the apps a lab runs, managed from Roostack. **Decided (2026-10-20), the Owner:** Roostack itself is the identity provider, and its OIDC part is written with the standard library; then, after the mock-ups, every recommendation below (groups in the first version, two-factor as for Roostack's own pages with a choice to ask everyone, "Keep me signed in" ticked for 30 days, the look on People) and a logo of either kind. The first version is built: see "As built (2026-10-20)" at the end of this section. The research behind it (every identity provider worth a look, and how each registry package can take single sign-on) is in [docs/SSO-RESEARCH.md](docs/SSO-RESEARCH.md).

**What the Owner asked for (2026-10-20):** one login for the lab's apps (Immich, Forgejo, Vaultwarden and so on); signing in to Roostack itself too, optionally; people, groups and who can use each service managed from Roostack; simple enough for an Owner who isn't a sysadmin; opting a service in should be a line in its package and a switch on its page, with Roostack registering the app, placing its secret and setting its addresses; and a sign-in page that looks like the lab's own (a logo, colours and a name, which the Owner can change).

#### What's there to build on

- **People:** Roostack's accounts (§5): argon2id passwords and TOTP in `authkit`, sessions with idle limits and "keep me signed in", rate-limited login pages, the audit log, an email address per person (for notifications, §6.13). No groups, and no per-person access to services: everyone signed in sees every service.
- **Caddy** is in front of every service, a pinned build of our own; `forward_auth` is a standard directive in it. Each service's address is generated by one function (`caddy.Site`), with the private/public rules, CrowdSec, rate limits and the WAF (§6.10, §6.11).
- **Packages** already template a route's address into env (`.Routes.<r>.URL`), pass generated secrets as env, and run install hooks inside a container (Forgejo's admin command already runs there).
- **Per-service switches** like the WAF's (`no_waf` in the instance file, then an apply) are the model for a single sign-on switch.
- **Backups** already carry each service's secrets and Roostack's database (§6.9).
- **Gaps:** the session cookie belongs to the UI's address only; nothing in a service's container can reach another address of the lab except through its public name (the "hairpin" back through Caddy, not yet tried); there is no lab logo, and themes are per person.

#### The choice: who signs people in

1. **Roostack itself — recommended.** Roostack is the identity provider: its own sign-in page, on an address of its own, `login.<domain>`, with two parts:
   - **OIDC**, for apps that support it (16 of the 23 packages);
   - **forward auth**, a wall in Caddy in front of apps that have no sign-in of their own, or that read who someone is from a header.

   Why:
   - one account and one second factor per person, the ones they already have;
   - the sign-in page is Roostack's, so it takes the lab's look from the design system with no extra work;
   - people, groups and access live where the Owner already manages people;
   - no extra container: nothing to update, nothing more in memory (the machines have 4–8 GB);
   - signing in to Roostack's own pages comes almost free later;
   - §5 asked for "Roostack to become the identity source later".

   The cost: Roostack takes on an OIDC provider, a security-sensitive piece it must keep right for good. It's kept narrow to limit that:
   - only the authorisation-code flow, with PKCE;
   - only apps Roostack registers itself (no self-registration, no implicit flow, no consent screens);
   - checked against the OpenID Foundation's conformance suite before release.
2. **Authelia, run by Roostack — the runner-up.** One small container (Apache-2.0, 4.39.28, active), forward auth and OIDC, configured entirely by files Roostack would write. People would still come from Roostack: it writes Authelia's users file from its own accounts (the password hashes are the same argon2id format). Why second:
   - **Its sign-in page can't take the lab's look:** a logo, and light or dark only.
   - **A second factor enrolled twice:** once in Roostack, once in Authelia, and enrolling in Authelia needs a code by email.
   - **Restarts sign people out:** adding an app means restarting it, which signs everyone out of walled apps unless a Valkey container holds the sessions.
   - **Its OIDC part is still labelled beta.**
   - **Roostack's own pages can't use it.**
3. **Considered:**
   - **Pocket ID:** the best API, but passkeys only and no passwords, by design, and no forward auth (a second component).
   - **Kanidm:** rigorous, but strict OAuth breaks several apps, it has no forward auth, and it wants TLS on its own port.
   - **VoidAuth:** people managed only in its own UI, one unaudited developer.
   - **Rauthy:** no TOTP, awkward forward auth, one developer.
   - **Dex:** its passkeys and TOTP are a fortnight old.
   - **Ory Hydra:** headless, and its open-source work has stalled since July.
   - **Zitadel, Authentik, Keycloak, Logto:** each needs Postgres and 0.5–2 GB.
   - **lldap:** a directory only, with nothing to add while Roostack holds the people.

**Decided (2026-10-20), the Owner:** (a) Roostack itself, as recommended. (b) The OIDC part is written with the standard library, as `awssig` and `authkit` were: about the size of the backup plan's code, with nothing new to vet or update, and the OpenID Foundation's conformance suite as the check. Considered: `zitadel/oidc` (well used and very active, but about fifteen new modules).

#### People, groups and access

- **Roostack holds the people.** Each person's username, name, email address and role are what apps see. A person needs an email address before they can be given a service with single sign-on (most apps match or make accounts by email). The address Roostack has for notifications is the one used; it is marked verified, because the Owner typed it in or the person changed it while signed in.
- **Groups (new):** named lists of people the Owner makes, such as "Family" or "Friends", on the People page. A person can be in several.
- **Who can use a service:** on the service's page, "Who can sign in": **Everyone in the lab** (the default), or chosen groups and people. Someone without access who tries sees a page saying so and who to ask, instead of the app.
- **Admins in the app:** where an app can make someone an admin from a claim (Mealie, Forgejo, Immich, Paperless, Audiobookshelf), Roostack's Owners and Admins with access are the app's admins, since they already manage services. Choosing others per service comes later.
- **Roles stay as they are** (Owner, Admin, Member): they say what someone may do in Roostack. Groups say which apps they use.
- **What an app is told:**
  - `sub`, a fixed ID per person that never changes, even if they're renamed or the lab moves;
  - with the `profile` scope, `preferred_username` and `name`; with `email`, `email` and `email_verified`;
  - with `groups`, `groups`, holding only `users`, plus `admins` for the app's admins.

  The lab's group names stay in Roostack; the app learns only what it needs.
- **Decided (2026-10-20):** groups in the first version, as asked. Considered: "everyone or chosen people" first.
- **Decided (2026-10-20), the Owner, after the conformance suite:** claims follow the scopes an app asks for, in its ID token and from userinfo alike (Mealie asks for `openid email profile groups`). Considered: every app told the same few facts whatever it asked for (as first built: simplest, but an app learns more than it needs, and the suite warns), and strict OIDC, with people's details only from userinfo (breaks apps that read them from the ID token alone).

#### Signing in

- **Where:** `login.<domain>` (a reserved subdomain, like `roost`). It serves only the sign-in pages and the OIDC endpoints; Roostack's own pages stay at `roost.<domain>`, private as before.
- **How:** the same sign-in as Roostack's (username or email, password, then the second factor where it applies), and passkeys there when Roostack has them (§5, "Passkeys later"; a new module, `go-webauthn`, to agree then).
- **Two-factor:** as for Roostack (Owners always; Admins while anything signs in from outside), and the Owner can require it of everyone.
- **Decided (2026-10-20):**
  - two-factor as for Roostack's own pages, with a setting to ask everyone (Settings → People → Signing in). Considered: requiring it of everyone signing in from outside home (safer, but family members must enrol);
  - how long a sign-in lasts: with "Keep me signed in" (ticked by default here, unlike Roostack's own pages) 30 days on that device, otherwise until the browser closes (and at most 12 hours). The apps keep their own sessions after that, as they do today.
- **Signing out** of the sign-in page ends the walled apps' access at once. Apps that use OIDC keep their own session until people sign out there; the page says so. Single sign-out across apps (back-channel logout) comes later.
- **Roostack's own pages:** first planned as a "Sign in with single sign-on" button on Roostack's login page, with the two sessions kept apart. Changed (2026-10-21), the Owner: signing in at `login.<domain>` should count for Roostack's pages too. How: the next section.

#### Signing in to Roostack's own pages — Locked (2026-10-21), as built the same day

**What the Owner asked (2026-10-21):** "If I sign in there, it should count as signed in for the control panel." One sign-in for the apps and for Roostack's pages, not a button and a second sign-in. It also makes the Immich photo page (§8.5, `roost.<domain>/immich-photos`, linked from the sign-in page) open straight away for someone signed in there.

**Decided (2026-10-21), the Owner:** one sign-in, as proposed below; Roostack's pages stay open as long as the sign-in for Members, while Owners and Admins keep Roostack's idle limit and then confirm with their two-factor code only; one sign-out.

**One sign-in, at `login.<domain>`.**

- **Going to `roost.<domain>` without a session** sends the browser to the lab's sign-in page (with the lab's look), and back. Someone already signed in there comes straight back, signed in to Roostack's pages too, with no page in between. They return to the page they asked for (an address on `roost.<domain>` only).
- **How the sign-in carries over: a one-time code**, as the walls do (`internal/sso/forward.go`). The sign-in page sends the browser back to `roost.<domain>/login/sign-in` with a code that lasts a minute and works once; Roostack swaps it for its own session cookie, on `roost.<domain>` only. To stop someone else's code signing a browser in as them (login CSRF), the trip starts on `roost.<domain>`, which sets a short-lived cookie the code is tied to.
  - Considered: **Roostack as an OIDC client of itself**, with a client, a secret and a token call. Both ends are the same process with the same database, so OIDC would add a secret, a network call and token checks for nothing.
  - Considered: **one cookie for the whole domain.** Already refused for the apps (§5.2, walls): every app would receive it.
- **The two sessions stay two rows, linked.** Roostack's session records the sign-in it came from:
  - **signing out** of either ends both (and the walls' access, as signing out of the sign-in page does now). Considered: signing out of Roostack's pages alone leaving the sign-in;
  - **the sign-in ending** (its 12 hours or 30 days) ends Roostack's session too;
  - **a person removed or given a new password** loses both, as now (a changed role takes effect at their next page, as now).
- **Two-factor:** the sign-in page already asks what Roostack asks (Owners always; Admins while Roostack's pages are public; everyone if the Owner chose so), so a sign-in there meets Roostack's rules. Someone the sign-in page turns away until they do something on Roostack's pages (a temporary password to change, two-factor to turn on) is sent on to Roostack's pages for that step alone, as Roostack's own login does now, instead of being stuck between the two.
- **How long Roostack's pages stay open** (Roostack's idle limit, §5, 30 minutes by default, and the sign-in page's length, 30 days with "Keep me signed in", ticked by default, differ):
  - **Members:** as long as the sign-in. Their pages hold nothing to protect beyond the sign-in page itself (their dashboard and their own account).
  - **Owners and Admins:** Roostack's idle limit, as on its own login. After it, the next page of Roostack's sends them to the sign-in page, which asks them to confirm it's them with their **two-factor code only** (the sign-in already had their password), then returns them to that page. An Admin without two-factor types their password instead. Their apps and their sign-in carry on meanwhile: an Owner's open settings on a forgotten laptop can restore backups or remove services, an app's page can't.
  - Considered: everyone as long as the sign-in (simplest, but an Owner's settings would stay open for 30 days on that device, "Keep me signed in" being ticked by default); everyone keeping the idle limit, Members too; asking Owners and Admins for their password and code again (as Roostack's own login does; the Owner chose the code alone, quicker, still needing their phone).
- **Roostack's own login page stays** for the ways in that aren't `roost.<domain>`: the SSH tunnel, a local address, a lab without an address, and as the way in if the sign-in page can't be reached. A sign-in there is Roostack's only, as today.
- **The sign-in page's home** lists Roostack's pages with the services (mock-up to follow).
- **The audit log:** "login" with "through the sign-in page" (the sign-in itself is already recorded as "signed in"); a code refused ("failed login", from where); signing out of both; and, for Owners and Admins, confirming with a code after the idle limit (and a code that didn't work).

**As built (2026-10-21):** `internal/web/pagessignin.go`.

- **The trip:** a page of Roostack's needing a session, at `roost.<domain>` over HTTPS while a service has single sign-on on, goes to `/login/sign-in`, which sets a cookie of its own (10 minutes, that path only) and sends the browser to `login.<domain>/roostack` with the cookie's hash and the page to return to. Signed in there, the browser goes back to `/login/sign-in/callback` with a code (in memory, a minute, once); Roostack takes it only with the matching cookie. `/login` itself goes on to the sign-in page there; Roostack's own login form stays at `/login?here`, and links to the sign-in page.
- **The link:** `sessions.sign_in`, a foreign key to the sign-in's row with `ON DELETE CASCADE`, so whatever ends a sign-in (signing out, its expiry, someone signing in as another person on that browser) ends the session made from it. The session expires with the sign-in, and its cookie outlives the browser only if the sign-in's does.
- **The idle limit:** a Member's session is stored as "keep me signed in" (no idle limit); Roostack checks the role at each page, so someone made an Admin since keeps the limit. Owners and Admins confirm when their sign-in's password was typed (`signed_in_at`) longer ago than the idle limit, so one who signs in for an app and opens Roostack's pages within it isn't asked twice; confirming doesn't change `signed_in_at`, which apps read as `auth_time` for a password.
- **Turned away by the sign-in page** (a temporary password, or two-factor still to turn on), someone on the way to Roostack's pages goes on there with a session of Roostack's pages alone (the code step first if they have two-factor), as Roostack's own login allows, and isn't signed in for apps.
- **Only once Caddy serves the sign-in page** (2026-10-21, found on VM2, docs/ASSUMPTIONS.md V17): a service has single sign-on on as soon as it's turned on, but an apply reloads Caddy last, after downloading images, and not at all if it fails; meanwhile `login.<domain>` doesn't answer. So Roostack asks its own Caddy (`127.0.0.1:443`, as monitoring does) for `login.<domain>/roostack/served`, which only its sign-in listener answers, with a token of that process; until the answer comes back, Roostack's pages keep their own login. The answer is kept a minute once yes, 5 seconds while no. Considered: reading the Caddyfile on disk (written at the start of an apply, before Caddy loads it), and a note from the apply when it reloads Caddy (misses a Caddy that failed to start, or was stopped).
- **Who can reach the sign-in page:** now also public while Roostack's own pages are (a lab on the internet whose UI isn't kept to an SSH tunnel), or someone outside couldn't sign in to them; a service's card says which.
- Tests: `internal/web/pagessignin_test.go` (the trip, a code used twice or in another browser, the Member's and the Owner's idle limit, an Admin's password, a temporary password, signing out each way, the ways in that keep Roostack's own login).


#### How each kind of app is connected

**OIDC apps.** When the Owner turns single sign-on on for a service, Roostack:

1. registers a client for it: an ID (the service's name), a generated secret, and its exact redirect addresses, made from the route's address and the package's paths;
2. gives the app its settings: the provider's address, the client ID and the secret (a Podman secret, as env, like any generated secret);
3. lets the app's containers reach `login.<domain>` from the inside (below);
4. restarts the service, and runs its single sign-on hook if it has one (Forgejo's `admin auth add-oauth`).

Turning it off does the reverse: the client and its secret go, and the hook undoes itself. A changed lab address updates the redirect addresses and the app's settings in the same apply.

**Walled apps (forward auth).** Caddy asks Roostack about each request to the service before passing it on.

- Someone not signed in is sent to `login.<domain>`, which sends them back with a one-time code. Roostack swaps the code for a cookie that belongs to **that service's address only**.
- Caddy removes that cookie before the request reaches the app, so a broken or malicious app never sees it.
- Signing out, or losing access, stops the next request.
- Apps that read who someone is from a header (Navidrome, Forgejo, Paperless) get `Remote-User`, `Remote-Email`, `Remote-Name` and `Remote-Groups`. Caddy removes any such headers a visitor sends, on **every** route, so nothing can pretend to be a signed-in person.
- Paths the package lists stay outside the wall (APIs used by phone apps, share links, status pages), each with the package's reason, and are protected by the app's own sign-in.

Considered: one cookie for the lab's whole domain, as Authelia and most walls do. Simpler, but every app would receive it and a compromised one could act as that person in every other app.

**Reaching the provider from inside.** An OIDC app's server swaps the code for a token by calling `https://login.<domain>` itself. Today that name leads only to the server's address on the home network, and whether a rootless container can reach its own server's Caddy that way isn't known. Proposed:

- Roostack adds `login.<domain>` to each opted-in container's hosts file (the Quadlet's `AddHost`), pointing at its server's Caddy as seen from the container (on the controller), or at the controller over the lab's WireGuard (on other servers).
- Caddy lets those addresses reach the sign-in host.
- The certificate is the lab's real one, so apps trust it.
- This is to be verified on a VM first (docs/ASSUMPTIONS.md).

A lab without an address yet (local `http://localhost` links) can't use single sign-on: the switch says to set the address first.

#### The package format

A new top-level part, `sso`, declares what the app supports. Each mode is optional. Roostack's checks list the redirect paths, the bypass paths and the hook like the parts they resemble (adding it touches the package's validation, which another session is editing now: to be done after that work lands).

```yaml
sso:
  oidc:
    route: web                                  # whose address the paths are on
    redirect_paths: [/login]                    # exact, on that address
    redirect_uris: [app.immich:///oauth-callback]  # other exact addresses (mobile apps)
    hook: sso                                   # optional: a hook run on turning it on, changing it or off
  forward_auth:
    route: web
    bypass_paths:                               # outside the wall, each with a reason
      - path: /api
        reason: Phone apps sign in with their own tokens.
  env:                                          # added to these containers only while it's on
    app:
      OIDC_AUTH_ENABLED: "true"
      OIDC_CONFIGURATION_URL: "{{ .SSO.DiscoveryURL }}"
      OIDC_CLIENT_ID: "{{ .SSO.ClientID }}"
      OIDC_ADMIN_GROUP: "{{ .SSO.AdminGroup }}"
  secrets:
    app: { OIDC_CLIENT_SECRET: sso.client_secret }
  note: Phone apps still sign in with the app's own password.   # shown on the switch
```

- **Env and secrets only while it's on:** the `env` and `secrets` in the `sso` part are added to the named containers only while single sign-on is on, and replace a container's own value of the same name (Stirling-PDF's `SECURITY_ENABLELOGIN`, Actual's login method). Several apps fail on an empty value, so a template that renders nothing isn't enough.
- **Template values**, in that env, in `when` and in hooks:
  - `.SSO.Enabled` (whether it's on);
  - `.SSO.Issuer` and `.SSO.DiscoveryURL`;
  - `.SSO.AuthorizeURL`, `.SSO.TokenURL` and `.SSO.UserinfoURL`, for apps without discovery (HedgeDoc);
  - `.SSO.ClientID`, `.SSO.ButtonText` ("Sign in with Smith family"), `.SSO.UserGroup` and `.SSO.AdminGroup`.
- **A hook** with `on: sso` gets `ROOSTACK_SSO=on|off` and the same values as env. It must be safe to run again (Forgejo: `auth list`, then `add-oauth` or `update-oauth`).
- **What the first version covers:** apps set by env (Vaultwarden, Mealie, Miniflux, Linkwarden, PhotoPrism, Actual, HedgeDoc, Stirling-PDF), by a command (Forgejo, Gitea), and walls (Stirling-PDF without accounts, Navidrome with headers). Uptime Kuma later: its wall also needs its own sign-in turned off, a setting in its database.
- **Later:**
  - apps whose secret sits inside a JSON value (Paperless, Papra): templated secret files;
  - apps set through their API with an admin key (Immich, Audiobookshelf, Wiki.js): a kept, generated admin key instead of today's install-only password.
- **Not sensible:** Plex, Jellyfin, ntfy, Radicale and Ghost; their packages say why.
- **An app's first admin:** where the install hook makes one (Miniflux, Actual), the package makes it with the installing Owner's username and email, so single sign-on later finds the same account rather than refusing it or making someone else the owner.

#### The UI

- **The service's page:** a "Single sign-on" row, like "Reachable from":
  - the switch;
  - "Who can sign in";
  - the package's note (what still uses the app's own password);
  - **Details**, with the client ID, the redirect addresses, the env given and the hook's log.

  Owners and Admins can turn it on (it changes a service, like the WAF switch); only Owners change who can sign in.
- **Installing:** a service whose package supports it has single sign-on on by default when the lab has it set up, so new apps start with it.
- **Settings → People:** groups; each person's services; the Sign-in page (below).
- **The dashboard** shows each person only the services they can use; Owners and Admins still see every service.
- **Mock-ups (2026-10-20):** [docs/mockups/sso](docs/mockups/sso/README.md): the sign-in page, Mealie's and Stirling-PDF's cards, and People with groups and the sign-in page's look.

#### The look of the sign-in page

The sign-in page uses the design system, so its look is a few lab-wide choices on Settings → People → Sign-in page:

- **the name:** the lab name (Settings → Lab name), already shown in the sidebar and on error pages;
- **a logo**, one of three kinds (the Owner, 2026-10-20: a mark with the name, or a full-width logo with the name in it). PNG or SVG, at most 200 KB.
  - **A mark, with the name beside it:** square, like an app icon, in place of the hen. It's also the page's icon in the browser tab.
  - **A wide logo, with the name in it:** shown on its own in place of the name, up to four times as wide as it is tall. The name is still the page's title and what screen readers say. Optionally, a version for dark mode; without one, the logo sits on a light panel when the device is in dark mode, so dark lettering stays readable. Optionally, a square icon for the browser tab; without one, the hen.
  - **None:** the hen, with the name.
- **colours:** one of the built-in themes and its accent (§3.2), so every choice passes the contrast checks in light and dark; the page follows the visitor's device for light or dark.

Later, the same choices could become the lab's default look for new people and Roostack's own login page. Considered: custom CSS (easy to break, and nothing a non-technical Owner would use); free colour pickers (can fail contrast).

#### Security

- **Who can reach the sign-in page:** it follows the services.
  - Private (home and VPN only, like the UI) while every opted-in service is private.
  - Public once one of them is public: it is useless otherwise, as people outside couldn't sign in to that service.
  - When public it has the same protection as a public service: CrowdSec, the WAF, and the strict login rate limit. The Owner sees that before making the service public.
  - Its routes reach only the sign-in pages and OIDC endpoints, never Roostack's own pages.
- **What a compromised app can do:**
  - It holds its own client secret, which can only ask for tokens for itself, for people who sign in to it.
  - Its tokens name it as their audience, so other apps refuse them.
  - It never sees the sign-in cookie or other services' cookies, and can't reach Roostack's own pages.
  - It learns the name and email of the people who use it, as today.
- **Secrets:**
  - **The signing key** (RSA, as almost every app expects RS256) lives in `/var/lib/roostack/sso/`, written 0600, and in the server's backup.
  - **Client secrets:** Roostack keeps only a hash of each; the secret itself is a Podman secret on the server that runs the service, and in its backup. For a service on another server, the controller makes the secret and sends it in that server's assignment over the lab API, like a secret someone typed (§10.3 otherwise keeps generated secrets on their server).
  - **Nothing is logged:** no codes, tokens or secrets (CLAUDE.md).
- **Tokens:**
  - codes last 1 minute and work once;
  - access tokens last an hour (Vaultwarden needs more than 5 minutes);
  - refresh tokens change at each use, and stop with the sign-in, the person's access, or the person.
- **The audit log** records turning single sign-on on or off, access changes, sign-ins to the sign-in page and refused attempts.

#### Backups, updates and failures

- **Backups:** groups, access, clients and sign-in sessions are in Roostack's database, and the signing key in `/var/lib/roostack`, both in the server's backup. A lab restored onto new hardware keeps its keys, so apps carry on without anyone signing in again. Each app's secret is in its service's backup, as now.
- **Updates:** nothing extra to update; it's part of Roostack.
- **When Roostack is down** (a restart during its own update, a few seconds):
  - walled apps show an error page ("Signing in is unavailable for a moment");
  - OIDC apps carry on for anyone already signed in, and only new sign-ins wait.
- **A lab moving to another address:**
  - redirect addresses and app settings follow in one apply;
  - people's `sub` stays the same, so apps find the same accounts;
  - apps that match on the provider's address as well need checking on a VM.

#### A first version, and what comes later

- **First:**
  - the sign-in page with the lab's look (name, logo, theme);
  - groups and per-service access;
  - the OIDC provider and the wall;
  - the `sso` part of the package format;
  - the switch on the service's page;
  - checked end to end on a VM with **Mealie** (OIDC, admin group) and **Stirling-PDF** (the wall), then Forgejo (a command).
- **Later:**
  - passkeys;
  - signing in to Roostack's own pages (built 2026-10-21, as one sign-in: "Signing in to Roostack's own pages" above);
  - apps set through their API (Immich, Audiobookshelf, Wiki.js) and secrets inside JSON (Paperless, Papra);
  - choosing an app's admins per service;
  - single sign-out;
  - signing-key rotation.

#### As built (2026-10-20)

Built and checked end to end on VM3 with Mealie (OIDC) and Stirling-PDF (the wall), and against the OpenID Foundation's conformance suite on VM4, docs/ASSUMPTIONS.md V1–V7.

- **The provider** is `internal/sso`, standard library only: an RSA key (`/var/lib/roostack/sso/signing-key.pem`, made at first start), RS256 ID tokens, discovery, `/authorize` (the code flow, by GET or POST; PKCE with S256 when the app sends a challenge, `plain` refused), `/token` (codes once each, a minute; refresh tokens rotated at each use, a used one working again for a minute (below); `client_secret_basic` or `client_secret_post`), `/userinfo`, `/jwks`, `/sign-out` (also apps' `end_session_endpoint`). Access tokens are opaque and stored (hashed) like refresh tokens, so they stop with the sign-in, the person's access or the person. Unit tests cover the flow and every refusal.
- **The OpenID Foundation's conformance suite** (release 5.3.3, Basic OP and Config OP plans, a static client) passes with no failures (2026-10-20, docs/ASSUMPTIONS.md V7; how to run it again: docs/SSO-CONFORMANCE.md). Its first run found, and these were fixed:
  - no `auth_time` in ID tokens: now always there, from when the person last typed their password (`sessions.signed_in_at`), and kept by a refresh;
  - `prompt=login` and `max_age` didn't ask for the password again: now they send the person back to the sign-in page (and `prompt=none` gets `login_required`). Signing in again as the same person keeps the sign-in and what it handed out, with a new time; someone else signing in on that browser ends the last person's sign-in;
  - an authorisation request posted from the app's page was refused as cross-site (OIDC Core §3.1.2.1 asks for POST too): `/authorize` takes it, as a link could send the same;
  - a code used twice didn't revoke the tokens it gave (RFC 6749 §4.1.2 asks that it should): a used code is kept until it expires, and a second try ends its access and refresh tokens;
  - every app was told every claim: they now follow its scopes (decided above);
  - discovery now says `request_parameter_supported`, `request_uri_parameter_supported` and `claims_parameter_supported` are false (the second is true when left out).

  **Decided (2026-10-20), the Owner:** a used refresh token works again for a minute (`sso.RefreshGrace`), for an app whose answer was lost (a timeout): its retry gets new tokens, and those the lost answer held are revoked. The minute counts from the first use, a different app's retry is refused, and so is any retry once the sign-in has ended. After the minute, a used refresh token is refused, as before. Kept in memory, like codes: a restart in that minute means the app signs in again. Considered: strict rotation, as first built (a dropped answer means signing in again); the grace plus revoking the newest tokens when an old refresh token comes back after it (stricter against a stolen token, more code); no rotation (RFC 9700 asks for rotation only for public clients, and every app here sends its secret, but a leaked refresh token would last until its sign-in ends).

  Left as they are, deliberately (the suite's warnings): no `given_name`, `birthdate` and the like with `profile` (Roostack doesn't hold them); `email` in the ID token with the `email` scope, as many apps read it there; no `acr` claim when `acr_values` asks (one way of signing in); the `claims` request parameter isn't supported. Request objects (`request`, `request_uri`) are refused with `request_not_supported`, which the suite accepts. The implicit and hybrid flows and dynamic registration aren't built, so their plans don't apply.
- **The sign-in page** is `internal/web/signin.go` and `templates/signin.html`, on its own listener, `127.0.0.1:8482` (`config.SignInPort`), which serves nothing of Roostack's own pages and no scripts at all. It answers its own host, Caddy's `/check`, and the walled services' `/.roostack/sign-in/callback`. Its session is a `sessions` row with origin `sign-in`, which Roostack's idle limit leaves alone. Signed in, the page itself lists the services you can use, and signs you out.
- **A walled service** gets a cookie on its own address after a one-time hand-off (`internal/sso/forward.go`). Caddy's wall is one `route` block (header removal, callback, `forward_auth` outside the bypass paths, the cookie removed before the service); `Remote-*` headers are removed on every route, walled or not.
- **The client secret** is a generated Podman secret, `roostack-<service>-sso-client-secret`, which the provider reads back (and keeps a few minutes) to check the app's requests: nothing new to store, and it's in the service's backup. Considered: a hash in the database (a second copy to keep in step).
- **Reaching the provider from inside:** `AddHost=login.<domain>:host-gateway` on an OIDC app's containers; Caddy sees them as `169.254.1.2`, which the private sign-in page lets in (`generate.ContainerHostGateway`, V1).
- **People:** `users.name` and `users.sso_id` (the `sub`: random, made when first needed, as `users.id` can be given to a new account after the newest is removed), `user_groups`, `service_access` (no rows: everyone), `sso_tokens`. People page: name, email and groups per person; groups with their members and the services each can use; the sign-in page's look; the two sign-in choices. Members' Home leaves out services with single sign-on they can't use.
- **The look:** the lab's name, a logo (a square mark beside the name, also the tab's icon; or a wide logo with the name in it, with an optional dark-mode version, otherwise a light panel in dark mode, and an optional tab icon; or the hen), and a built-in theme. PNGs are checked for shape; SVGs with scripts, event handlers or embedded pages are refused, and every logo is served with a sandboxing CSP. Mock-ups: docs/mockups/sso.
- **The package format** has `sso` (oidc or forward_auth, env and secrets added only while on, a note) and `.SSO` in templates (help/docs/reference/packages.md). Mealie and Stirling-PDF have it in the test fixtures (`internal/testdata/packages`), a revision ahead of the registry.
- **Not built in this version** (TASKS): the `on: sso` hook (Forgejo, Gitea), apps set through an API with a kept admin key (Immich, Audiobookshelf, Wiki.js), secrets inside JSON (Paperless, Papra) (all three built the same day: "Connecting the remaining apps" below), services on another server (refused with a sentence for now), passkeys, signing in to Roostack's own pages (built 2026-10-21, above), single sign-out, key rotation. Removing a service leaves its access rows (harmless; tidied once `services.go` is free).
- **Found while building:** a custom `dns.directive` without a DNS challenge (`issuer internal`) made Caddy refuse the Caddyfile, as `resolvers` needs one; resolvers are now added only with a `dns` line.

#### Connecting the remaining apps — Locked (2026-10-20)

The first version connects apps set by env, and walls. Three ways of connecting an app were designed above but not built. This is how each works.

**Decided (2026-10-20), the Owner:** every recommendation below, as proposed: the lab's name on Forgejo's button, a key the app makes kept from a hook's output, a Connect action for services installed before their package had one, and Forgejo's source kept on turning off when accounts were made through it.

Facts behind it, read from each app's source at the pinned version (not yet tried on a VM):

- **Forgejo 15:** `forgejo admin auth add-oauth` and `update-oauth` (which changes only the flags given). `auth list` prints `ID`, `Name`, `Type` and `Enabled`, separated by tabs.
  - There is no flag to turn a source off.
  - `auth delete` refuses a source that any account was made through ("login source is still used by some users").
  - The button says "Sign in with <the source's name>", and the callback is `/user/oauth2/<the name, escaped>/callback`.
- **Immich 3.3:**
  - Its OIDC settings are part of its whole server configuration (`GET` and `PUT /api/admin/config`, all of it at once).
  - API keys can be limited to what they may do: `adminConfig.read` and `adminConfig.update` are enough.
  - A key belongs to an Immich account, here the administrator the install hook makes. It is made by `POST /api/api-keys` with that account's sign-in.
  - Its image has `node` and `curl`.
- **Audiobookshelf 2.37:** an API key acts as the account it's made for, with all of that account's power. Its sign-in settings take a `PATCH`.
- **Wiki.js 2.5:** its API must be turned on first. A key has full access, or a group's. Setting a sign-in method replaces the whole list, so a hook must read the list and send `local` back.
- **Paperless-ngx 3.3:** its OIDC provider is a JSON value, `PAPERLESS_SOCIALACCOUNT_PROVIDERS`, with the client secret inside. Its image reads any `PAPERLESS_*_FILE` variable from a file.
- **Papra 26.7:** its providers are a JSON value, `AUTH_PROVIDERS_CUSTOMS`, secret inside. It has no `_FILE` variables, but takes a config file.

##### 1. The `on: sso` hook

**The format.** A hook with `on: sso`, like `on: install`: no name, run in order with any others. The `hook:` field sketched under `sso.oidc` above is dropped: one way to say it, not two.

```yaml
hooks:
  - on: sso
    title: Connect Forgejo to the lab's sign-in page
    container: app
    env:
      SOURCE_NAME: "{{ .SSO.ProviderName }}"
      DISCOVERY_URL: "{{ .SSO.DiscoveryURL }}"
      CLIENT_ID: "{{ .SSO.ClientID }}"
      ADMIN_GROUP: "{{ .SSO.AdminGroup }}"
    secrets:
      CLIENT_SECRET: sso.client_secret        # empty while single sign-on is off
    command:
      - sh
      - -c
      - |
        set -e
        id_file=/var/lib/gitea/roostack-sso-source     # in Forgejo's own data, so it's backed up with it
        id=$(cat "$id_file" 2>/dev/null || true)
        if [ "$ROOSTACK_SSO" = off ]; then
          [ -n "$id" ] || { echo "Nothing to undo."; exit 0; }
          if forgejo admin auth delete --id "$id" 2>err; then rm -f "$id_file"; echo "Single sign-on removed."
          elif grep -q "still used" err; then echo "Kept: accounts were made through it. Its button now says single sign-on is off." >&2
          else cat err >&2; exit 1; fi
          exit 0
        fi
        set -- --name "$SOURCE_NAME" --provider openidConnect --key "$CLIENT_ID" --secret "$CLIENT_SECRET" \
          --auto-discover-url "$DISCOVERY_URL" --scopes openid --scopes email --scopes profile --scopes groups \
          --group-claim-name groups --admin-group "$ADMIN_GROUP"
        if [ -n "$id" ] && forgejo admin auth list | awk -F'\t+' -v id="$id" 'NR > 1 && $1 == id { f = 1 } END { exit !f }'; then
          forgejo admin auth update-oauth --id "$id" "$@"; echo "Single sign-on updated."
        else
          forgejo admin auth add-oauth "$@"
          forgejo admin auth list | awk -F'\t+' -v n="$SOURCE_NAME" 'NR > 1 && $2 == n { print $1 }' > "$id_file"
          echo "Single sign-on added."
        fi
```

Forgejo's `sso` part gains `env` for its container while on: `FORGEJO__oauth2_client__ENABLE_AUTO_REGISTRATION: "true"`, `ACCOUNT_LINKING: auto` (an existing account with the same email is linked, not refused), and `USERNAME: nickname` (which reads `preferred_username`). Its redirect path names the source, so redirect paths may use `.SSO` templates and a new `pathescape` function: `/user/oauth2/{{ pathescape .SSO.ProviderName }}/callback`. Renaming the lab then renames the source and its redirect address in one apply. Gitea is the same.

**What it gets:**

- `ROOSTACK_SSO=on` or `off`, set by Roostack;
- the env and secrets the hook lists, as for any hook. `.SSO` values are there whether it's on or off, so an `off` run can find what to undo;
- `sso.client_secret` as a hook secret, for an OIDC app only. It is empty in an `off` run.

**When it runs.** After each apply on the server that runs the service, once the service has started and its install hooks are done. Roostack keeps a fingerprint of the hook's last successful run in its database (`hooks.<service>.sso`): a SHA-256 of on or off, the hook's command, its rendered env, and the names of its secrets (never their values). It runs the hook when that fingerprint changes. So it runs:

- when single sign-on is turned on or off;
- when what it was given changes: the lab's address or name, or a package update that changes the hook;
- after installing, for a service installed with single sign-on on (the default).

An `off` run happens only after an `on` run did. A service that never had single sign-on runs nothing.

**As whom, and where its log shows.** As install hooks do: `podman exec` into the named container, as the container's own user. It's retried every 10 seconds for up to 10 minutes while the app starts. It shows as an activity, "Connecting Forgejo to the sign-in page", in the activity log. The service's Single sign-on card shows its last run under **Details**, with the time.

**On failure:**

- The service keeps running, and the switch stays as chosen.
- The card says "Forgejo isn't connected to the sign-in page yet", with the last lines of output and **Try again**.
- A failed `on` run means the app's button may be missing, or may still carry the old settings.
- A failed `off` run leaves a button that leads to the sign-in page. For a service without single sign-on, the sign-in page answers "Single sign-on is off for Forgejo: sign in with its own password", with a link back. This is new, and better than the bare "unknown client" error any leftover would get today.

**Turning it off cleanly.** The apply removes the app's sso env and client, then the hook runs with `off`.

- Forgejo deletes the source, or keeps it if accounts were made through it (Forgejo refuses otherwise).
- Accounts made through single sign-on have no password of their own. The switch's confirmation says so: "People who signed in with single sign-on will need a password: give them one with Reset a password".
- The client secret is deleted once the `off` run succeeds, as §5.2 says ("the client and its secret go"). The first version leaves it in Podman.

**The rules a package must follow.** `on: sso` needs an `sso` part. It takes no `name` or `inputs`. `sso.client_secret` is allowed only with `sso.oidc`. Install-only secrets aren't allowed: they're gone by then. A walled app may have one too: Uptime Kuma's wall needs its own sign-in turned off, and back on when the wall goes.

##### 2. Apps set through their own API: a key the app makes, kept by Roostack

**The credential.** Immich, Audiobookshelf and Wiki.js make their own keys: none takes a key Roostack chooses. So the app makes it, and a hook hands it to Roostack:

- a **secret setting with `from_hook: true`**: its value is what a hook prints, never typed by anyone nor made by Roostack;
- a hook with **`saves: <that setting>`**. What it prints on **standard output** becomes the secret, and nothing else. Its standard error is its log.

```yaml
settings:
  - key: settings_key
    title: Roostack's key to Immich's settings
    description: Made by Immich at install, for setting up single sign-on. Never shown.
    type: secret
    from_hook: true

hooks:
  - on: install                      # the administrator, as today (unchanged)
    ...
  - on: install
    title: Make a key for Roostack
    container: server
    env: { ADMIN_EMAIL: "{{ .Settings.admin_email }}" }
    secrets: { ADMIN_PASSWORD: admin_password }   # still there: install-only secrets go once all install hooks succeed
    saves: settings_key
    command: [node, -e, "...sign in; delete any key named Roostack; POST /api/api-keys {name: 'Roostack', permissions: ['adminConfig.read', 'adminConfig.update']}; print its secret, and only it, on stdout"]
  - on: action
    name: connect
    title: Connect Roostack to Immich
    description: Roostack needs a key to Immich's settings to set up single sign-on. Type Immich's administrator email and password once; Roostack keeps only the key.
    container: server
    inputs:
      - { key: email, title: Administrator email, type: string }
      - { key: password, title: Administrator password, type: secret }
    env: { ADMIN_EMAIL: "{{ .Inputs.email }}" }
    secrets: { ADMIN_PASSWORD: inputs.password }
    saves: settings_key
    command: [node, -e, "...the same script"]
  - on: sso
    title: Set Immich's sign-in
    container: server
    env:
      ISSUER: "{{ .SSO.Issuer }}"
      CLIENT_ID: "{{ .SSO.ClientID }}"
      BUTTON: "{{ .SSO.ButtonText }}"
    secrets:
      API_KEY: settings_key
      CLIENT_SECRET: sso.client_secret
    command: [node, -e, "...GET /api/admin/config; on: oauth = {enabled, issuerUrl, clientId, clientSecret, buttonText, autoRegister: true, scope: 'openid email profile groups', roleClaim: 'groups'}; off: oauth.enabled = false, clientSecret = ''; PUT it back"]
```

Immich makes someone an admin when a claim it's told to read holds `admin`. Audiobookshelf reads `admin` or `user` from its group claim, and refuses anyone with neither. Today's `groups` claim holds `users` and `admins`. So **a package may rename the two groups** the provider sends it: `sso.oidc.groups: { users: user, admins: admin }`. `.SSO.UserGroup` and `.SSO.AdminGroup` follow. Considered: a role claim of its own per package (one more claim to build, for the same result).

**How it runs.** A saving hook runs like any hook, with three differences:

- Its standard output is read by Roostack and never shown or logged. It must be one line of 1 to 4096 printable characters; anything else fails the hook with "printed something that isn't a key", without showing it.
- The value replaces the secret (`podman secret create --replace`), so a new key takes over from the last one. The scripts delete their old "Roostack" key first, so the app holds only one.
- It runs only when its own event comes: at install, or the action's button.

**Where the key lives, and its power:**

- A Podman secret, `roostack-<service>-<key>`, on the server that runs the service. It's in the service's backup, like every secret, so a restored Immich keeps working.
- Never shown: `from_hook` can't be `reveal`. Never in a container's env: only hooks may use it. Never logged.
- **Immich's key** can read and change Immich's server settings, and nothing else: no photos, no accounts. Changing settings is still a lot of power (pointing Immich's sign-in somewhere else would let that place sign in as anyone), so it's kept like an admin password.
- **Audiobookshelf's key** is the administrator's own power, and **Wiki.js's** is full access: neither app offers less. The package's install summary and its `from_hook` setting's title say so.
- What can read it: the roostack user, which already holds every service's secrets and data. A compromised app holds its own key only while a hook runs in it, and it already has its own data.

**Existing installs, and a lost key.** A service installed before its package had a key, or whose key Immich lost (its administrator was deleted, say), has the `connect` action. The Single sign-on card offers it in place of the switch while the key is missing: "First connect Roostack to Immich: this needs Immich's administrator password once."

- A package whose `sso` hook uses a `from_hook` secret must have an action that saves it. The install hook that saves it is recommended but optional.
- With the key missing, the `sso` hook fails, saying so.

**Considered:**

- **A Roostack account in each app, with a generated password Roostack keeps:** works for every app. But it's full admin power, it shows in the app's list of people, and it can be used from anywhere the app is reachable.
- **Writing the app's database directly** (Immich keeps its settings in `system_metadata`): no credential at all, but it bypasses the app's checks and caching, and breaks when the app changes its tables.
- **Immich's config file:** it freezes every other setting in Immich's pages.

No new image is needed: Immich, Audiobookshelf and Wiki.js all have `node` in their own images.

##### 3. Secrets inside JSON: an env value Roostack makes as a secret

**The format.** `sso.secret_env`: per container, a variable and a template for its whole value. In it, `.SSO.ClientSecret` is available. A new `json` function (also in every other template) writes a value as a JSON string, quoted and escaped, since the lab's name may hold quotes.

```yaml
sso:
  oidc:
    route: web
    redirect_paths: [/accounts/oidc/roostack/login/callback/]
  env:
    app:
      PAPERLESS_APPS: allauth.socialaccount.providers.openid_connect
      PAPERLESS_SOCIAL_AUTO_SIGNUP: "true"
      PAPERLESS_SOCIAL_ACCOUNT_SYNC_SUPERUSER_GROUP: "{{ .SSO.AdminGroup }}"
  secret_env:
    app:
      PAPERLESS_SOCIALACCOUNT_PROVIDERS: >-
        {"openid_connect": {"SCOPE": ["openid", "profile", "email", "groups"],
         "APPS": [{"provider_id": "roostack", "name": {{ json .SSO.ProviderName }},
                   "client_id": {{ json .SSO.ClientID }}, "secret": {{ json .SSO.ClientSecret }},
                   "settings": {"server_url": {{ json .SSO.DiscoveryURL }}}}]}}
```

**How it runs.** It works as `databases.<name>.url` does today, which is made from the database's password:

1. The generator renders the template with a placeholder for the secret. It never has the secret.
2. The reconciler makes a Podman secret from that text, putting the client secret (JSON-escaped) in the placeholder's place.
3. The container gets the secret as `Secret=…,type=env,target=PAPERLESS_SOCIALACCOUNT_PROVIDERS`.

The secret's name ends in a short hash of the rendered text (without the secret). A changed lab address or name then gives a new name, so the Quadlet changes and the app restarts with the new value. Roostack removes the service's secrets of that kind that it no longer uses.

**Where the secret goes:** only into Podman's secret store, where every other secret already is. Never into a Quadlet, unit, Caddy file or any file Roostack writes. `.SSO.ClientSecret` exists only in `secret_env` templates: anywhere else, the package check refuses it.

Papra is the same, with `AUTH_PROVIDERS_CUSTOMS` (no `_FILE` needed, as Podman passes it as env).

**Considered:**

- **A wrapper around the image's entrypoint** that builds the JSON from a mounted secret file: each image's entrypoint and shell would have to be known and kept in step, and Papra's rootless image may have no shell.
- **Paperless's `_FILE` variables:** these only move the whole value into a file, which Roostack would still have to make. A Podman secret passed as env does the same with nothing on disk.
- **Papra's config file:** written by Roostack into the app's folder, with the secret in it.

**One thing to check on the VM:** Paperless gives an admin from the group claim only when an existing account signs in. So someone's first sign-in may make an ordinary account, and their second makes them an admin. If so, the package's note says so.

##### What else changes

- **The package editor** follows every change:
  - help for `on: sso`, `saves`, `from_hook`, `secret_env` and `sso.oidc.groups`;
  - the same checks in `check.js`, with corpus cases;
  - the hook in "Settings and setup", and `secret_env` and the claims in "Single sign-on";
  - suggestions: an OIDC app with no env and no `sso` hook, an `sso` hook using a `from_hook` key with no action to make it, a `from_hook` setting no hook saves.
- **The registry's packages:** Forgejo, Immich and Paperless-ngx first; then Gitea, Audiobookshelf, Wiki.js and Papra the same way.
- **Hook output:** before logging or showing a hook's output, Roostack replaces any secret value it passed to the hook with `[secret]`. A careless script can then not leak one. This applies to every hook, not only these.

##### The questions the Owner answered (2026-10-20)

1. **Forgejo's button:** "Sign in with Smith family" (the lab's name, recommended), with redirect paths able to use `.SSO` and the hook keeping the source's ID in Forgejo's data folder? Or a fixed name, "Sign in with single sign-on", which is simpler but reads oddly?
2. **The kept key:** an API key the app makes, saved from a hook's output (`from_hook` and `saves`, recommended)? Or a Roostack account in each app with a password Roostack generates (one way for every app, but full admin power and visible in the app)?
3. **Existing installs:** a "Connect Roostack to Immich" action that asks the app's administrator password once (recommended)? Or only new installs get single sign-on for these apps, as nothing is released yet?
4. **Turning off when accounts were made through it:** keep Forgejo's source, with its button leading to a page saying single sign-on is off (recommended)? Or switch the source off directly in Forgejo's database (cleaner to see, but it bypasses Forgejo and may break with its next version)?

##### As built (2026-10-20)

Built as decided, and checked end to end on VM2 (arm64, the test address and Caddy's own CA as for the first version) with the registry's new revisions, Forgejo 15.0.10-roostack.2, Immich 3.3.3-roostack.2 and Paperless-ngx 3.3.2-roostack.2 (docs/ASSUMPTIONS.md V8–V10). Each was signed in to through the lab's page by a Member (a new account in the app) and by the Owner with two-factor, then turned off.

- **The package format:**
  - `on: sso` hooks;
  - `saves` on install hooks and actions, and `from_hook` secret settings;
  - `sso.secret_env`;
  - `sso.oidc.groups`;
  - redirect paths with templates;
  - the `json` and `pathescape` template functions.

  The rules are in `pkgspec` (`validate.go`, `sso.go`), mirrored in the package editor and its corpus.
- **Running sso hooks** is `internal/node/ssohooks.go`. After each apply, a service whose fingerprint changed runs its hooks, once its install hooks are done, retried like them. The record (on or off, the fingerprint, any failure, the last output) is a runtime setting, `hooks.<service>.sso`. A failed run waits for Try again (the existing setup retry), not for the next apply. Hooks' output, for every hook, has the secrets they were given replaced by `[secret]`. `podman exec`'s standard output and error now come back apart (`podman.RunSplit`), so a saving hook's key never reaches a log.
- **Turning off** deletes the client secret once the hooks have run, and any secret env, for every OIDC app, hooks or not. (The first version left the client secret in Podman.) A secret env made for an earlier address is deleted the same way while it's on.
- **Found on the VM, and fixed:**
  - *A kept button failed.* Forgejo, with its source kept, answered its button with a 500 error, not the "single sign-on is off" page: with it off, its container had no route to the sign-in host, and the lab no sign-in site. An OIDC app with an sso hook now keeps its `AddHost` while it's installed on a lab with an address, on or off, and the lab keeps its sign-in site (private) while any such app is installed (`generate.SSOReaches`). The button then leads to "Single sign-on is off for Forgejo. Sign in with Forgejo's own password instead." An app set by env (Mealie) loses both when it's off, as before.
  - *The install form asked for the key.* A `from_hook` key was shown as a secret to type. It's now never asked for, taken from a form, or made by the reconciler, and its absence is no error. It's still listed among the service's secrets, so backups and moves keep it once it exists.
- **The Single sign-on card** shows a running hook (polling), a failure with Try again, or the Connect action when a key is missing, and the last run under Details. `?action=<name>` opens an action on the service's page, so Connect can link to it. The install summary lists the keys an app makes ("Key: Roostack's key to Immich's settings").
- **Paperless-ngx doesn't link an existing account by email.** Its administrator, made at install with the Owner's email, got Paperless's "finish signing up" form, as allauth has no setting Paperless exposes for that. The account is connected from its own profile page, signed in with its password once; the package's note says so. Whether a new account gets superuser at its first sign-in or only its second (Paperless syncs groups on an existing social account's sign-in) wasn't checked: the Member was the only new account.
- **Test only, on the VM:** each app was made to trust Caddy's own CA by an extra variable in a copy of its package (`SSL_CERT_FILE`, `NODE_EXTRA_CA_CERTS`, `REQUESTS_CA_BUNDLE`), as Mealie was for the first version. A lab with a real certificate needs none of them.

##### Gitea, Audiobookshelf, Wiki.js and Papra (2026-10-20)

New registry revisions use the same three ways (roostack-services `claude/sso-more-apps`), checked end to end on VM2 (docs/ASSUMPTIONS.md V11–V14):

- **Gitea 28.1.0**, as Forgejo:
  - Its `update-oauth` sets skip-two-factor whether given or not, so the hook always passes `--skip-local-2fa`.
  - It reads `preferred_username` itself.
  - `ACCOUNT_LINKING=auto` links an existing account by username first, then email.
  - Gitea syncs admin from the groups claim at each sign-in, so an admin made in Gitea who isn't a lab Owner or Admin loses it at their next single sign-on (the package says so).
- **Audiobookshelf 2.37.1**, as Immich:
  - Its key can't be narrowed: it has its administrator's whole power, which the setting's description and the install summary say.
  - Its own discovery (`/auth/openid/config`) fills in the endpoints.
  - Its root administrator, made at install, has no email, so it's found by username (`authOpenIDMatchExistingBy: username`).
  - The groups are renamed `user` and `admin`, which it reads.
- **Wiki.js 2.5.316**, as Immich, through GraphQL:
  - Its API is turned on for the key, which has full access.
  - Every existing sign-in method is sent back with the lab's, as `updateStrategies` replaces the whole list.
  - Off disables the lab's method rather than deleting it, so the accounts it made aren't orphaned.
  - Wiki.js doesn't read the provider's configuration, doesn't ask for groups, and never links an account it has already. So everyone who signs in gets an account of their own, the lab's Owners and Admins too, and Wiki.js's administration stays with the account made at install.
  - **Decided (2026-10-20), the Owner:** they join a group named after the lab that can read and edit pages. The hook makes it the first time (or takes the one a failed run made), then only renames it, so changes made in Wiki.js stay. *Considered:* read only (Wiki.js's Guests).
- **Papra 26.7.0**, as Paperless-ngx, with its provider list in `secret_env`.
  - Papra makes an account through sign-in only while sign-ups are on, which would also let anyone who reaches it sign up with a password. It links an account it has already only if that account's email is verified, which the install-made one isn't.
  - **Decided (2026-10-20), the Owner:** single sign-on only. While it's on, sign-ups are on and Papra's email-and-password sign-in is off (`AUTH_PROVIDERS_EMAIL_IS_ENABLED=false`), so nobody can sign up any other way. The account made at install can't be used until it's off again, which the package's note says. *Considered:* sign-ups on beside password sign-in (fine while it's home-only, open to anyone otherwise), and no single sign-on for Papra yet.
