- **Found on the VM:** Wiki.js wants a group's permissions and page rules as lists, not JSON strings. A failed run had already made the group, so a retry now takes a group of that name rather than making a second.

##### The rest of the registry (2026-10-21)

**Asked by the Owner:** everything working before merging.

New revisions are on the same registry branch, checked end to end on VM2 (docs/ASSUMPTIONS.md V15, V16):
- Mealie and Stirling-PDF, as in the test fixtures (checked for the first version, V3, V4);
- Vaultwarden, Miniflux, Linkwarden, Actual Budget and HedgeDoc, all set by env;
- Navidrome, behind the wall with header login.

`requires.roostack` stays `"0.1"`: no release has been tagged, the first will be v0.1.0, and development builds run every package. So it's right as long as these branches are merged before v0.1.0 (TASKS).

- **What each needed:**
  - **Vaultwarden:** `SSO_AUTH_ONLY_NOT_SESSION`, so it keeps its own sessions, as the sign-in page's access tokens last an hour. The master password still unlocks each vault.
  - **Miniflux:** finds accounts only by the provider's ID. Its install-made administrator is linked from its own Settings, and until then signing in through the lab with that username is refused ("This user already exists").
  - **Linkwarden:** stores an email only when it sends mail, so its install-made administrator can't be linked. It keeps its password, and the lab's sign-in with the same username is refused (`OAuthCreateAccount`).
  - **Actual Budget:** the first person to sign in becomes its owner, after typing the server password once. Turned off, Actual still lists OpenID as its method, and the server password works with Password on its sign-in page.
  - **HedgeDoc:** every address is given, as it has no discovery. Accounts are keyed by `sub`.
  - **Navidrome:** reads `Remote-User` (below). `/rest` (Subsonic apps) and `/share` stay outside the wall.
- **Navidrome trusts the header only from its own address.**
  - Requests Caddy forwards arrive at a container from its own address (rootless port forwarding).
  - Another container reaching it directly arrives from its network's gateway.
  - So Navidrome starts through a one-line shell that sets `ND_EXTAUTH_TRUSTEDSOURCES` to its own address (`/32`) while single sign-on is on.
  - With the range open, a forged header from another app's container made Navidrome create that account. With its own address only, the forged header was ignored.
- **Found on the VM, fixed the same day:** a container could reach another service's container across their Podman networks. One app's container fetched Navidrome's directly, so a compromised app could bypass a wall (and Caddy) to reach another service. Each service's network is now isolated (§6.4, "Each service's network is isolated"); Navidrome still trusts the header only from its own address, as a second layer.
- **Decided (2026-10-21), the Owner:**
  - **Not for now:** Uptime Kuma (its login can be turned off only over its live connection with its account password) and PhotoPrism (its free edition makes people who sign in guests, and won't link its admin).
  - **Later:** Lychee (it knows only a few named providers, none at our addresses) and Ghost (it would need a custom adapter).
  - **Not sensible:** Jellyfin, Plex, ntfy and Radicale. Their own apps sign in themselves, and Radicale's header login would leave its CalDAV paths open.

## 6. Container Runtime — Locked (principle)

- Podman, **rootless where possible**; rootful only where a service genuinely requires it (e.g. low ports, host networking, device access), and this is visible to the user.
- **Rationale:** security is a priority; rootless limits the blast radius of a compromised container.

### 6.1 Roostack Process Architecture — Locked

Roostack runs **directly on the host** (not in a container), as two processes shipped in one `.deb` package:

| Component | Runs as | Responsibilities |
|---|---|---|
| `roostack` | Unprivileged `roostack` user, strict systemd sandboxing | Web UI, API, desired state, rootless service management (Quadlets, status, logs) |
| `roostack-agent` | root, in a systemd sandbox | A small fixed set of privileged operations only: OS updates, reboots, the firewall, WireGuard, backup drives (listing, mounting, preparing blank disks) and folders, the host time zone, SSH's keys-only setting (§6.10). Listens on a local socket that only root and `roostack` may use, from the host's own user namespace. (Rootful Quadlets were planned here; nothing needs them yet.) |

- Nodes in a multi-server lab run the same package (§10).
- Roostack updates itself through apt, alongside host OS updates (§3.2), from its own apt repository (§7.2).
- **Installing it — as built (2026-10-18):** one command, `curl -fsSL https://dl.roostack.cloud/install.sh | sudo sh` (`scripts/install.sh`, addresses confirmed by the project's Owner on 2026-10-18; the key's hash and the suite are filled in at the first release). The script is short so it can be read first, downloads nothing but the release key, and does exactly the help's manual steps: checks Debian 13 on amd64 or arm64; fetches the key and checks the **SHA-256 of the whole key file** against one written into the script before installing it as `/usr/share/keyrings/roostack-archive-keyring.gpg`; writes `/etc/apt/sources.list.d/roostack.sources` (deb822, `Signed-By` that keyring only, the current minor series' suite); `apt-get update` and `apt-get install -y --no-install-recommends roostack` (Podman's recommended extras left out, about 300 MB: Roostack depends on the ones it uses, `aardvark-dns` and `ca-certificates` included, since 2026-10-18). The package ships the same two files and keeps them up to date from then on. Everything runs from a function called on the last line, so `curl | sh` can't run half a script, and apt can't swallow its rest from standard input. `ROOSTACK_REPO`, `ROOSTACK_SUITE` and `ROOSTACK_KEY_SHA256` point it at a test repository (plain HTTP allowed only then). **Why the file's SHA-256, not the OpenPGP fingerprint:** a fresh Debian 13 has no `gpg` to print one (apt checks signatures with `sqv`), and pinning the whole file also refuses a second key added to it; the help's manual steps show the fingerprint for people with `gpg`. A re-exported key (a new expiry) means a new `install.sh`, published with it. Considered: the key inside the script (no download at all; kept as a separate file so the manual steps and the script fetch the same thing).
- `roostack` is hardened with systemd sandboxing (`ProtectSystem`, `ProtectHome`, `NoNewPrivileges`, `PrivateTmp`, system call filtering, limited writable paths) to get most of a container's isolation.
- **`roostack` never runs `podman` directly — Locked (2026-09-26).** Rootless Podman needs the setuid `newuidmap`/`newgidmap` helpers, which `NoNewPrivileges` disables. So `roostack` only talks to the `roostack` user's systemd manager (`systemctl --user`, `systemd-run --user`), which runs Podman outside the sandbox. One-off commands (creating secrets, database dumps, `podman unshare` for backups) go through `systemd-run --user`.
- **Rationale:**
  - Keeps root code small and easy to review; privileged operations are explicit, not implied by broad socket access.
  - Podman/Quadlets rely on host systemd for lifecycle, so a containerised manager would need root-equivalent host access anyway.
  - Two components, a standard Debian install and update path, and no "container updating itself" problem.
- **Considered:**
  - *Container + many host mounts (Portainer/Komodo style):* those tools rely on the Docker daemon socket, which is root-equivalent. Under Podman they would also need the Quadlet directory, systemd D-Bus access, and a root agent regardless.
  - *Container web UI + host agent (A′):* a valid design that isolates the web-facing part. Rejected for now: it means either much more root code or three components, and the gain is limited because the agent API must allow arbitrary service deployment. The roostack↔agent API boundary leaves this option open later.
  - *Single root process:* rejected; the web UI would run as root.
- This deviates from PLAN.md's original "roostack orchestration container" wording. Services still run in containers; Roostack itself does not.
- **Root never works in the roostack user's folders — as built (2026-10-16).** `/var/lib/roostack` and `/srv/roostack` belong to the `roostack` user, so anything running as it (the service, or something that broke out of a container) can put a symlink there, and root following it would write wherever it points (`fs.protected_symlinks` only guards world-writable sticky folders like `/tmp`). Found in the security review of 2026-10-16 (H6). So:
  - **Commands that work on Roostack's files run as the `roostack` user, even with `sudo`.** `roostack user`, `roostack backup` and `roostack apply` started as root run themselves again as `roostack` (its UID, groups and systemd session) before reading anything. `roostack node join` does the join as `roostack` (its files and the agent calls; the agent accepts `roostack`) and keeps only the restart of the service as root. The process run as `roostack` gets no terminal: it runs in a session of its own and its output comes back through pipes with control characters removed, since another `roostack` process could take it over and type into root's terminal (TIOCSTI, which Debian 13's kernel turns off anyway) or send it escape sequences. A side effect: `sudo roostack backup snapshots` now works without `sudo -u roostack XDG_RUNTIME_DIR=…`. And since 2026-10-19, `sudo -u roostack roostack …` works as it is: run as the roostack user without that user's session (`sudo -u` passes none on, `su` keeps root's), the CLI sets `XDG_RUNTIME_DIR` and the D-Bus address itself (`ownSession`); root and other users are left as they are. *Considered:* `runuser`/`setpriv` (the same, through another program; Go's `os/exec` sets the user itself), and opening every file with `O_NOFOLLOW` as root (every path in the code, easy to miss one).
  - **The database refuses symlinks.** SQLite follows a symlink for the database file itself, and the modernc driver can't pass `SQLITE_OPEN_NOFOLLOW`; it opens `-wal` and `-shm` without following (docs/ASSUMPTIONS.md S1). `store.Open` refuses any of the three that isn't a plain file.
  - **Drives (review M17, M18):** what `lsblk` says about a drive comes from the drive, so a crafted USB stick chooses its label, model and UUID, and lsblk's JSON brings back a newline in a label (docs/ASSUMPTIONS.md S5). The agent drops control characters from names, accepts only UUIDs shaped like one (8-4-4-4-12 hex, XXXX-XXXX or 16 hex digits), and refuses an fstab line with a field holding whitespace, a control character or `#`. It erases only a disk with no filesystem backups could use, nothing mounted and nothing in `/etc/fstab` (by UUID, PARTUUID, label or device): an idle backup drive is only automounted, so "not mounted now" let the roostack user erase it. So "Erase it and start afresh" for a drive Roostack could use is gone (the Owner's choice, 2026-10-16); erase it elsewhere first. *Considered:* also allowing drives with a filesystem that aren't in fstab (the roostack user could still erase any other drive plugged in), and the Owner's confirmation passed to the agent (the agent can't tell it from a request the roostack user makes up).
  - **The agent's inputs (review Lows):** WireGuard keys must be exactly what base64 writes for 32 bytes (Go's decoder skips newlines, which would then reach a wg-quick file inside a key). A typed backup folder must exist already: the agent makes only the `roostack` folder inside it (it used to make any missing folders on the way, owned by `roostack`), in a handle on the folder opened through an `os.Root`, opens it with `O_NOFOLLOW` and changes its owner through that handle (`fchown`), so a symlink swapped in midway is never followed.
  - **The agent's sandbox:** `roostack-agent.service` lets it write only to `/etc/sysctl.d`, `/etc/wireguard`, `/etc/roostack`, `/etc/ssh/sshd_config.d` (added 2026-10-17, §6.10 item 3), `/mnt`, `/media` and `/srv` (`ProtectSystem=strict`), with Unix and netlink sockets only, a capability bounding set of what its jobs use (`CAP_NET_ADMIN`, `CAP_SYS_ADMIN` for partition tables, `CAP_CHOWN`, `CAP_DAC_OVERRIDE`, `CAP_DAC_READ_SEARCH`) and `@system-service` system calls. OS updates and reboots run in transient units outside it. Its socket moved to `/run/roostack-agent/agent.sock`, its runtime directory, since `/run` is read-only to it. Every job was checked on a VM (docs/ASSUMPTIONS.md S7).
  - **Editing `/etc/fstab` (2026-10-22, quality review P5):** every change goes through one `editFstab`: under a lock (the agent serves requests at the same time, and two edits at once lost one), written only if it changed, then systemd reloads.
    - **Replaced whole, in one step (the Owner's choice, 2026-10-22).** The agent writes the new file to `/etc/roostack/fstab.new` (synced) and keeps the one before as `/etc/roostack/fstab.previous`, for anyone fixing a server by hand. A short systemd job (`systemd-run`, as OS updates run) then runs `roostack-agent commit-fstab`, which renames it over `/etc/fstab` and syncs `/etc`: both are on the same filesystem, so a power cut leaves the old file or the new one, never part of one (a rename across filesystems fails rather than copying). If any step fails, `/etc/fstab` is as it was.
    - **So the agent no longer writes `/etc/fstab` itself:** it left `ReadWritePaths`, which only narrows the sandbox. It had to: that entry bind-mounts the file into the agent's namespace, so after a rename outside it the agent would have gone on seeing the old file; without it, the agent reads `/etc/fstab` through its read-only view of `/etc`, which sees the new one at once (docs/ASSUMPTIONS.md S8). Starting a `systemd-run` job widens nothing: the agent could already.
    - *Considered:* writing in place, synced (a power cut mid-write could leave fstab empty or short, and the server not booting properly); `ReadWritePaths=/etc` for the rename (the agent, root with `CAP_DAC_OVERRIDE`, could then change any file in `/etc`); a last good copy put back by the agent (too late: systemd reads fstab before the agent starts).
  - **Only callers from the host's own user namespace:** `SO_PEERCRED` can't tell a rootless container's root (the `roostack` UID to the kernel) from the service, so a container given the socket in a bind mount (a package of the Owner's own can mount one; the registry's policy refuses it) could call the agent (docs/ASSUMPTIONS.md S6). The agent now refuses callers whose user namespace isn't the host's: their `uid_map` must map every UID to itself, read through a pidfd so a reused PID can't pass. Agreed with the Owner (2026-10-16). *Considered:* documenting it and relying on the package policy (H2 showed it can be bypassed).
  - **The installer:** `postinst` makes the folders inside `/var/lib/roostack` and `/srv/roostack` only when nothing is there (on every upgrade, `install -d` would have followed a symlink there and given its target to `roostack`, docs/ASSUMPTIONS.md S2), and the install message reads the setup code as `roostack`, keeping only the characters a code has.

### 6.2 Language: Go — Locked

- Both `roostack` and `roostack-agent` are written in Go, in a single codebase.
- **Rationale:** Builds a single static binary, which makes `.deb` packaging trivial. Low memory use suits NUC-class hardware. Podman is written in Go and there are mature systemd/D-Bus libraries. The language is small, readable and widely known, which fits the simplicity principle. The maintainer wants to learn it.
- **Considered:** Rust (steeper learning curve, slower to build with); Python (easiest to write, but harder to package and ship as a host service).

### 6.2.1 Releases: signed and reproducible — as built (2026-10-18)

Part of "encryption anyone can check" (TASKS): what a lab runs should be checkable against the published source, not taken on trust. Details and the key's ceremony: docs/RELEASING.md.

- **A release is a tag** `vMAJOR.MINOR.PATCH` on `main`. Pushing it runs `.github/workflows/release.yml`, which builds both packages (amd64, arm64), the plain `roostack` binaries (for the registry's Publish workflow, §8.3.3) and `SHA256SUMS`, signs `SHA256SUMS`, and hands the files to `apt-publish.yml`, which publishes them at `dl.roostack.cloud/releases/<version>/` and in the apt repository. Not GitHub's releases: the repository is private.
- **Reproducible:** the same tag builds the same bytes anywhere. `scripts/build-release.sh` (`make release`) pins the Go release (the go tool fetches it), uses `-trimpath` and an empty build ID, ignores the builder's go settings, and builds the help site with the Zensical in `uv.lock`; `build-deb.sh` dates every file with the commit's time, sorts, and compresses on one thread. The workflow builds in a Debian 13 image pinned by digest, twice (two runners, two folders), and stops unless both builds are identical. Considered: comparing only the binaries (the .deb is what servers install, so it must match too); Ubuntu's own tools on the runner (they change with the runner image, so a later rebuild might not match).
- **Signed with one OpenPGP key** (Ed25519): the primary key certifies only and stays offline; a signing subkey, without a passphrase, is the secret of a GitHub environment `release` that waits for a reviewer. The same key signs the apt repository, and the package installs its public half as apt's keyring, so updates to the key reach labs with Roostack's updates. Considered: a passphrase on the CI copy (it would sit beside the key in the same environment, protecting nothing more); the primary key in CI (a leak would mean replacing the key on every lab); Ed25519 signatures with the registry's tooling (apt checks OpenPGP).
- **Anyone can check a release — as built (2026-10-19):** `make verify-release VERSION=x.y.z` (`scripts/verify-release.sh`) takes the published `SHA256SUMS` and its signature, checks the signature against the release key the tag has (or one given) and shows its fingerprint, rebuilds the tag in the image the tag's `release.yml` pins, with its uv, and compares every file's SHA-256, ending with a plain "matches its source" or "DOES NOT MATCH" and the files that differ. It needs only git, curl and Podman or Docker; the signature is checked in the container before anything is built. Go comes from Debian's `golang-go` fetching the release's Go through `GOTOOLCHAIN` (checked against Go's checksum database), not from a download checked against a hash in the repository; on an arm64 VM it matched a release built with Go from go.dev, so either way gives the same bytes. Considered: checking only the binaries (the .deb is what servers install); a rebuild on the host (its dpkg-deb and xz would differ).
- **Guards:** no release without the registry's real root key (§8.3.3) or with a development build, from a changed checkout, from a tag `git describe` doesn't give, from a commit not on `main`, or while the committed public key is the placeholder.

### 6.3 Web UI: Go templates + htmx + Alpine.js — Locked

- Server-rendered HTML, embedded in the `roostack` binary. No JavaScript build step.
- Step-up approach for interactivity. Use the lowest level that works:
  1. **htmx** for server interactions (default)
  2. **Alpine.js** for small in-browser behaviour (toggles, show/hide, simple validation)
  3. **Standalone JS libraries** for heavy widgets (e.g. xterm.js for a terminal, a chart library)
  4. **A separately built component** only when a feature genuinely needs it, decided case by case
- **Architecture rule:** business logic lives in core Go packages. HTTP handlers are a thin layer on top. This keeps the UI replaceable and supports the master/worker API and a possible CLI.
- **Rationale:** Roostack's UI is mostly forms, lists, status and logs, with state held on the server, which is htmx's strength. Validation lives in one place (Go). A small toolchain suits the simplicity principle and a maintainer learning Go.
- **Rejected:** React islands. They bring back a Node build toolchain and a second UI paradigm for limited gain.
- **Known limits:** UIs with a lot of browser-side state (drag-and-drop dashboards, visual editors) would need step 4.
- **Live updates — as built (2026-09-29):** parts of pages that show changing state (the home page, Services, the Backups status card, the drive list) poll with htmx, and are kept cheap: they poll **only while the tab is visible** (and refresh once when it becomes visible again); each poll sends a fingerprint of what the part shows, and the server answers **204 No Content** when it's unchanged, so htmx leaves the page alone (nothing to download or redraw; open panels, selection and scroll stay put); the controller **shares its reads of other servers** for 10 seconds, so several open tabs cost one round of calls (any change forgets them); and responses are **gzip-compressed** (a page is about a third of its size). Activity logs poll every 2 seconds only while something runs. **Later:** push instead of poll, once Roostack moves to htmx 4, with its WebSocket support (`hx-ws`); see TASKS.
- **Sizes — Locked (2026-09-28):** shown in decimal units everywhere (1 GB = 1,000,000,000 bytes; kB, MB, GB, TB), as drive makers, macOS and most people count them, so an "8 GB" USB drive reads 8.0 GB. One formatter (`internal/units`) serves the UI, logs and the CLI, and sizes people type (e.g. a storage quota in GB) use the same units. Binary units (GiB) were considered: exact, but unfamiliar to most people, and a drive would read smaller than its label.
- **Settings in the sidebar — Changed (2026-10-19), the Owner's choice:** Settings is a main item in the sidebar like Services, opening the overview; while you're in Settings every settings page is listed under it (each with its icon), and nowhere else. It replaces a Settings heading that wasn't a link, a short list of everyday pages and "All settings" at the bottom. Pins, recently opened pages, folding groups and a filter box were mocked up and not chosen (docs/DESIGN.md "Layout", docs/mockups/settings-nav).
- **The setup wizard shows only itself — decided (2026-10-18) by the project's Owner, as built:** while first-run setup lasts, its steps (two-factor, where the server is, who can log in, the lab's address with Roostack Cloud's linking pages, backups and the recovery key) have no sidebar or app navigation: the plain page of the Welcome page, with the brand mark, "Setup / Step N of 5", the step's help and Log out. Found in an end-to-end test that day: the steps showed the whole app around them, and two-factor showed as a page of the Owner's account (with its tabs) that went to Home afterwards, so it looked as if setup had ended. **Two-factor is the wizard's first step** (`/setup/two-factor`) for the Owner while the wizard lasts; the code goes on to the next step, and `/account/2fa` sends the Owner there meanwhile. Otherwise (other people, or an Owner whose two-factor was reset after the wizard) two-factor is the account's page as before. Once the wizard is over, the same pages under Settings have the sidebar. *Considered:* keeping the sidebar with its links hidden (the app's frame stays, but it still looks like the app rather than a setup that has to be finished first).

### 6.3.1 Help site — Locked (2026-09-29)

- **What:** a help site for the person running Roostack, in plain language, with a collapsed **For technical users** section on each page (exact paths, commands, logs), plus a technical reference (files, command line, ports, package format). Source in `help/` (Markdown), separate from `docs/`, which stays developer-facing.
- **Tool: [Zensical](https://zensical.org)** (from the Material for MkDocs team), pinned exactly in `help/pyproject.toml` and `uv.lock` (pre-1.0, so releases may break), run with `uv run zensical`. Chosen by the Owner of the project.
- **Where it's read:** both **inside Roostack** at `/help` (built at `make build` and embedded in the binary, so it works offline and matches the installed version; the UI's "Tell me more" links can point into it) and as a **public site** (address to be decided; `help.roostack.cloud` for now). Pages use relative links, so one build serves both.
- **Look:** Roostack's "Teal and violet" colours (`help/docs/stylesheets/roostack.css`, kept in step with `themes/teal.css`), the Roostack small mark (`help/docs/assets/logo.svg`, generated by `assets/brand/generate.py`), light/dark following the device. No web fonts or anything loaded from other sites.
- **In Roostack — as built (2026-09-29):** `make build` runs `zensical build --strict` (a broken link fails it) and copies the site to `internal/web/helpsite/site`, which is embedded; only a README there is committed, so `go build` and the tests work without Python, and `/help/` then says the help isn't included. Without `uv`, `make build` warns and carries on; `scripts/build-deb.sh` refuses to package a build without the help. `/help/` is served to anyone, logged in or not (it's public anyway, and "Locked out" must be readable before logging in). The top bar has **Help**; the login page links to "Locked out". Considered: committing the built site (every help edit would change ~60 generated files).
- **Page help — as built (2026-09-29):** every page with a matching help page (or section) links to it, top right above the title, on the back link's row: a question-mark icon and the help page's title, opening in a new window like **Help** (the `help` component). A test checks each page and `#section` exists in `help/docs`. Considered: a "Tell me more" link in each page's text (the UI already uses "Tell me more" for details that open in place; mixing the two would surprise people), and a link at the end of each page (easy to miss).
- **Its Content-Security-Policy — Locked (2026-09-29):** `/help/` gets its own policy; the rest of the UI keeps its own. **Scripts:** only the pages' inline scripts, by SHA-256 hash, worked out from the embedded files when Roostack starts (so they always match the build). **Styles:** `'unsafe-inline'`, because Zensical's search box injects a stylesheet held in its minified script, and pages have style attributes; hashing that stylesheet would mean picking a string out of minified code, which would break silently with a new Zensical. Inline styles can't run code, and the help has nothing written by users to inject into. Browsers ignore `'unsafe-inline'` if a hash is also listed, so there are no style hashes. Rejected: `'unsafe-inline'` for scripts.
- **The public site — Locked (2026-10-12):** at `help.roostack.cloud`, on Cloudflare (static files), deployed from this repository's `zensical build` output; the website, roostack.cloud, is hosted the same way. Considered: a path under roostack.cloud (`/help`; the two sites are built and released from different repositories), and Caddy on a server of our own (one more thing to keep up).
  - **The preview — as built (2026-10-14):** `scripts/help-deploy.sh` builds the help (strict) and publishes it with Wrangler (run with `npx` at the website's pinned version, so this repository gains no Node dependency) to `roostack-help-preview` on workers.dev, from `help/wrangler.jsonc`. It writes `site/_headers` first: the same Content-Security-Policy as the help inside Roostack (scripts only by SHA-256 hash, worked out from the built pages; inline styles allowed for the search box), HSTS, nosniff, and `X-Robots-Tag: noindex` while it's a preview. Checked under `wrangler dev` (pages, search, the 404 page, no CSP violations) and live. `--build-only` builds without publishing. The website's preview links to it. For help.roostack.cloud, `help/wrangler.jsonc` has a `production` environment (the custom domain) and `scripts/help-deploy.sh --production` publishes it without `noindex`; Roostack Cloud's `ops/site-deploy.sh` runs it with an API token, never a personal login. Prepared 2026-10-14, not yet published.
- **Considered:** plain MkDocs with Material (Zensical is its successor, by the same team, and reads the same Markdown); writing help pages as Go templates inside the UI (no search, and no public site without Roostack running).

### 6.3.2 Reaching the UI — Locked (2026-09-30), first run from the home network (2026-10-18)

- **The UI listens on `127.0.0.1:8480` only** (apart from the setup pages during first-run setup, below). With a roost.ac name, it is served at the name itself (`https://smiths.roost.ac`, §6.6); with an own domain, at `https://roost.<domain>` (named `roostack.<domain>` until 2026-10-01; shortened by the project's Owner) through Caddy, to the home network only (private). Without one, it's reached over an SSH tunnel (`ssh -L 8480:127.0.0.1:8480 …`), which works the same on Windows 10/11 (PowerShell), macOS and Linux, but is a hard first step for people who don't use a terminal, hence the setup pages on the home network below. The installer shows the command with the server's address filled in; on a server joining an existing lab, it points to `sudo roostack node join <code>` first, which needs no setup page.
- **First-run setup from the home network — Decided (2026-10-18), as built:** until setup is finished, Roostack also serves its setup pages, and only those, over **HTTPS on port 8443 to the home network**, with a **self-signed certificate** made at its first start (`/var/lib/roostack/setup-tls.crt` and `setup-tls.key`, 0600). The installer's message and the log (`journalctl -u roostack`) show the address by IP (`https://192.168.1.20:8443`), the certificate's **SHA-256 fingerprint** to compare with the browser's warning, and the setup code. The SSH tunnel still works, and the installer shows it as the other way. **Why:** the tunnel needs a terminal, which the people Roostack is for often don't use; a phone or laptop on the same network is what they have. **Rejected:** plain HTTP (considered 2026-09-30): the setup code and the Owner's password would cross the network readable by anyone on it; listening publicly; a real certificate (no name exists yet; Let's Encrypt's IP certificates need the server reachable from the internet).
  - **How long — decided (2026-10-18) in the security review of this work, confirmed by the project's Owner the same day:** until the wizard is finished (its last step, or setup finished over a tunnel, checked every 30 s), not only until the Owner exists, so the Owner isn't left mid-wizard needing the tunnel after all; and **never more than 24 hours after the Owner was made**, finished or not. The 24 hours count from the account's `created_at` in the database, so a restart doesn't start them again. Considered: stopping at the Owner (the rest of the wizard over the tunnel). The wizard's last step shows **Setup is finished** on the listener with where Roostack is from now on (the lab's address, or the tunnel); past the 24 hours, a page asked before the listener notices (within half a minute) shows **This setup page has closed** with the tunnel command (setup carries on there where it was left); after that the port is simply closed, and the help says why. Then the listener stops, the port closes, the certificate is deleted and everything signed in on it is signed out; from then on it's `127.0.0.1:8480` and Caddy as before. A server joining a lab, or a restored lab, never opens it again (its certificate and sessions are deleted at the next start).
  - **What it serves, and to whom** (`internal/web/firstrun.go`): only the first-run routes, even to the Owner: the first-run pages and wizard (two-factor set-up is its first step, `/setup/two-factor`; the account's `/account/2fa` isn't served here), logging in, and what the wizard's pages load or post to (its address step's Cloud link and network choice, the backup step's live log, a backup destination's location), the help and static files; everything else is 404. Before the Owner exists, the setup code guards them. **Once the Owner exists, every route needs the Owner signed in on the listener**, and only the wizard's two-factor step and the help until it's on (the help is public on Roostack's own pages, and the step links to it: since 2026-10-18); anyone else gets the login page (and its static files), and the setup code no longer works there.
  - **Its sessions and cookies work only on it:** a session records where it was signed in (`sessions.origin`: `setup` on the listener, empty on Roostack's own pages), and each is refused on the other, so a cookie the browser also sends elsewhere (cookies are per host, not per port) gets nothing there, and one from Roostack's own pages gets nothing on the listener. The same holds for the setup code's 30-minute pass, a login waiting for its two-factor code and a lab restore's search (kept in memory, tagged with where they were made); the listener sets no "known device" cookie and offers no "keep me signed in". When the listener stops, all of them are revoked (and again at Roostack's next start, if it stopped with Roostack).
  - **Its last page (as built, 2026-10-18; found unstyled the same day):** once setup is finished every page there is "Setup is finished", except its own stylesheets and scripts (`/static/`), which still load. The listener closes 5 seconds after the wizard's end (`SetupListener.Finish`, called by it, so the page has time for its files), and the restart the wizard postponed waits for the listener to close (at most 30 s): it used to come a second after the last step, before the browser asked for the page's stylesheet. The page says that reloading it won't work. Considered: the stylesheet inline in the page (a second copy of the design system's CSS to keep), and a longer fixed delay before the restart (a guess either way).
  - **A lab restore started on the listener (as built, 2026-10-18):** the restore ends with Roostack restarting as the restored lab, which closes the listener (the restored lab's Owner is older than 24 hours, or its wizard is done), so the progress page can't follow it to the login page as it does over the tunnel. Instead, on the listener, the finished restore says that this setup page closes, and where to go: the lab's address from its restored settings, if it has one that opens from home, and the SSH tunnel with the server's address filled in. The same words are in the page from the start, hidden, and show if the page stops getting answers before it saw the end (the restart came first). *Considered:* keeping the listener open for a while after a restore (it would serve the restored lab's login to the home network, which the 24-hour rule is there to stop).
  - **Linking to Roostack Cloud from the listener** (the address step's roost.ac name): the lab asks Cloud to send the browser back to the form's `Origin`, here `https://<home address>:8443/setup/address/linked`. Acceptable: the address carries no secret (Cloud shows the place it's sending the browser to first), Cloud learns only a private address it can't reach, the page there needs the Owner's session on the listener (a top-level navigation, so the `SameSite=Lax` cookie goes with it), and if the listener has closed meanwhile the browser just can't load it: linking finishes by itself on the lab's side either way, and the Owner carries on over the tunnel.
  - **The firewall:** roostack-agent opens 8443 to the home network (`trust.HomeRanges`, the `lan4`/`lan6` sets) only while setup lasts (`Firewall.SetupPort`). Before the Owner has chosen the firewall (the wizard's SSH step), Roostack used to leave it alone entirely; now its table is there during setup with only that rule (`Firewall.Unchosen`: 8443 from the home network accepted, from anywhere else dropped, everything else accepted as before), and goes when setup is finished. The listener also closes connections that aren't from a home address to a home address before TLS, in case the firewall hasn't been applied yet. So a cloud server with only a public address never serves it: its installer message shows the tunnel.
  - **Someone else on the network setting it up first:** they'd need the setup code, which is only on the server (the installer's message, the log, a 0600 file). Its checks are unchanged: compared in constant time, half a second's wait after a wrong one, a 30-minute cookie once right, and checked again when the account is made.
  - **Someone in the middle** (ARP or DHCP spoofing) could show their own certificate: browsers warn about a self-signed certificate either way, so the installer and the help ask people to compare the fingerprint before going on. Someone who skips that check is no worse off than over plain HTTP, and Roostack can't do better without a name.
  - **DNS rebinding:** a page on another site whose name now points at the server can't complete a TLS handshake (the certificate isn't for its name, and scripts can't click through a warning), and the setup listener answers only requests whose `Host` is an IP address, never a name (421 otherwise). It doesn't require the server's own address: a port forward or a VM's network changes it on the way (found on the dev VM), and a page can only make a browser send an address it was itself opened at, which then goes to that address.
  - **Cross-site forms (CSRF):** as on the rest of the UI, a form must carry an `Origin` matching the `Host` (so it came from the setup page itself), and the setup code's cookie is `SameSite=Strict`. Nothing the setup listener receives is trusted as coming from a proxy (`X-Forwarded-*` are dropped).
- **Considered (2026-09-30), replaced by the above (2026-10-18):** serving the setup page to the home network over plain HTTP, only on a server with no public address. Rejected then and now: an option to listen publicly.
- **Open, for later: HTTPS by IP address.** Let's Encrypt issues certificates for bare IP addresses (short-lived ones), which could give a cloud server's setup page `https://<its address>` with a real certificate and no domain. Check whether Caddy can get one and how it renews, once public access has settled.
- **On a lab on the internet, the UI is public — Decided (2026-09-30) by the project's Owner, as built:** at `roost.<domain>`, as a public route: CrowdSec's bouncer (CrowdSec runs whenever the UI is public), 1000 requests per 10 s per visitor, and 10 a minute on `/login` and `/backups/recovery-key` (not `/setup`: the setup code can't be guessed, and the wizard's pages are under it). 2FA is required for Owners, and for Admins while the UI is public (§5). The Owner can keep it to an SSH tunnel instead (`public.ui_tunnel_only`), in the wizard's first step or Settings. Considered: tunnel only (safest, but the Owner of a cloud lab would need a terminal for everything).
- **Open: serving the UI through the domain as an explicit choice (at home).** At home, a domain automatically serves the UI at `roost.<domain>` (home network only). Proposed by the project's Owner (2026-09-30): make that something the Owner turns on once a domain is set. Questions: what is the UI reached by if it's off (the tunnel only?); does the wizard offer it at the domain step. (On the internet it's the choice above.)

### 6.3.3 Installing, step by step — Locked (2026-09-30); retries and restarts as built (2026-10-18)

- **Why:** a first install can take minutes (downloads, and on a brand-new server Caddy's image build), and the UI showed "stopped" with an **Open** link, or nothing at all, so people couldn't tell whether it had hung. Agreed with the project's Owner from a mock-up (2026-09-30).
- **As built (2026-09-30):** a new service (all its containers new) shows **installing** until its last step is done. Its page lists each step (done, in progress with "for …", or to do): *Downloading* and *Starting* each container ("the database (Postgres)", "the cache (Valkey)", or the service), *Making it reachable at its web address* (or *Waiting for Roostack's web proxy to finish building* while Caddy's image builds; left out while Caddy waits for the domain step), and *Finishing setup* if the package has install hooks. The log is under **Details** (the apply's, then the setup's). The Services list and Home show "Step 3 of 5: …" and no **Open** link; the address is shown greyed until it's running. Before its apply begins (e.g. waiting for another change), it shows one step, *Waiting for other changes to finish*. After 5 minutes on one step, the notice says it's taking longer than usual and when its log last changed. When it finishes, the page reloads to show it running.
- **How:** the node tracks installs in memory (`node.InstallProgress`, part of `ServiceDetail`, so other servers' installs show too), fed by the reconciler's progress events. Each new container's image is pulled first as its own step (`podman pull` through `systemd-run --user`, output in the apply log), so the download isn't hidden inside the unit's start (and its 5-minute pull timeout under systemd doesn't apply). New services start before a new Caddy, whose first start waits for its image build, so downloads run while it builds.
- **A failed install, and a restart halfway (as built, 2026-10-18):** a failed download or start shows **Try again** (Admins) beside the error, which applies again at once (`RetryInstall`, the same route as a failed setup's); the next apply or restart also tries again. The installs are kept in the server's settings (`installs`), not only in memory, so one cut off by a restart of Roostack still shows its steps afterwards. In both cases the containers' Quadlet files are already written, so a plain apply would only start them, with no download step; instead the node tells the reconciler which containers' first start didn't finish (`Reconciler.Unfinished`: every container of an install with a download or start step not done), and they're started as new ones again: downloaded first (quick if the image is there: `podman image exists`) and started one at a time, step by step. An install whose service has left the server is forgotten.
- **Not done:** download progress in MB. Without a terminal, `podman pull` prints a line per layer ("Copying blob …") and no progress (checked again on Podman 5.4, 2026-10-18); its progress comes only through Podman's API socket (a service Roostack doesn't run), or by watching the size of its temporary files, which the sandboxed daemon can't see. Left until there's a reason to run Podman's API.

### 6.3.4 Error pages — Locked (2026-10-13)

- **Why:** a stopped service showed a browser's blank error or Caddy's empty reply, an unknown address showed nothing at all (the connection was dropped), and Roostack's own errors were a line of plain text. People couldn't tell whether the lab was broken, the service was stopped, or the link was wrong.
- **Decided with the project's Owner, from mock-ups (2026-10-13):** each page has a picture, a joke headline and plain words underneath saying what happened and what to do. The picture is a *scene*: the small mark's hen and server (or, for Roostack Cloud, the hen on a cloud), without its rounded tile, on a soft wash that fades out, with a few props (a nightcap for a stopped service, an egg hatching for one starting). Considered: the tile kept as a frame (felt like an icon, not a scene); plain icons without the mark (less recognisably Roostack).
- **As built (2026-10-13):**
  - `assets/errors/generate.py` draws the scenes and writes `internal/errpage/scenes.go` and a standalone SVG of each (in the default theme's colours) for Roostack Cloud's own pages. Colours are `es-*` classes, so in the UI they follow the person's theme.
  - **Roostack's pages:** 404 *This page has flown the coop*, 403 *That's above your pecking order*, 500 *Roostack scrambled that one* (`internal/web/errors.go`). Handlers keep using `http.Error`; a wrapper swaps a plain-text 403, 404 or 500 for the page when a browser opens a page (GET, not htmx), so parts of pages still get the short answer they expect.
  - **The services' pages (Caddy):** 404 (no service at this address), 403 (refused by CrowdSec, below), 429 (rate limited), 502 (not answering), 503 (starting; the page reloads itself every 10 seconds), 504 (too slow) and any other error. Caddy's `handle_errors` serves them from `/etc/caddy/errors` as templates, written with the rest of Caddy's files. Each route sets its service's name, title and whether it's private in `vars`, so the page says "Forgejo is asleep on the job".
  - **What a page tells a visitor:** only a private service's page (the home network, or the lab's own network) names the lab, links to the service in Roostack and has **Details** (status, Caddy's message, the time). A public service's page shows the joke and the plain words only: an outside visitor needn't learn the lab's name or where its UI is. Considered: the details everywhere (useful, but tells strangers about the lab).
  - An unknown address under the lab's domain now gets the 404 page instead of a dropped connection. A private service opened from outside is still dropped (§6.6): answering would show that something is there.
- **Refused requests — as built (2026-10-19):** CrowdSec's bouncer answered a banned address, and a request AppSec refused (§6.10 item 4), with a bare "403 Forbidden", so a visitor (or the household from outside, after a false positive) saw a blank page. Now the bouncer hands its refusals to Caddy as errors (`enable_caddy_error`), and `handle_errors` shows `403.html`, which has two versions, chosen by Caddy's template. The bouncer gives both the same error, so each public route sets `roostack_waf` just before AppSec checks a request (`caddy.WAFVar`): with it set, AppSec refused it, *That one ruffled some feathers.* (it looked like an attack; if you were only using the service as usual, tell whoever runs it, as the check sometimes gets it wrong); without it, the address is banned, *Shooed out of the henhouse.* (turned away for a while, after it was seen probing or attacking websites, this one or others; if that wasn't you, someone on the same network may have been: try again later, or from another connection). Its scene is `shooed` (a no-entry sign, a broom). The page repeats nothing from the request (not even the host), names no lab (only public routes refuse) and has no "Try again" (the same request is refused again). The bouncer warns that its bare answer is cheaper under a flood; this page is a static template like the other error pages, which anyone could already get (an unknown name gets the 404 page), so it adds no new work an attacker can cause. *Considered:* one page giving both reasons (vaguer, for one line per route saved); the old 403 page, whose words were about too many requests.

### 6.4 Service Execution: Podman Quadlets — Locked

- Roostack generates **Quadlet** files (`.container`, `.volume`, `.network`, etc.) and systemd runs them. Rootless units go in `~/.config/containers/systemd/`.
- Users never write Quadlets directly. They are generated from Roostack's own service definitions (see §8).
- **Rationale:**
  - Official Podman feature (4.4+), maintained by the Podman project, and an open, well-supported format.
  - systemd provides start-at-boot, restart-on-failure, dependency ordering, and logging (`journalctl`) with nothing extra to build.
  - Designed for rootless operation (§6).
  - The generated files are plain text a homelabber can inspect (Principle 4), and they are declarative (Principle 2).
- **Rejected:** podman-compose. It is community-maintained, supports only part of the Compose spec, and needs extra pieces for boot and restart handling. Also rejected: running docker-compose against the Podman API socket, which brings back a daemon-style model with the same lifecycle gaps.
- Rootless services need `loginctl enable-linger` for the service user so they start at boot without anyone logging in.
- **Each service's network is isolated — as built (2026-10-21):** every service has its own Podman network, `roostack-<service>`, with all of its containers and databases on it (packages can't choose another, §8.3.4). Its `.network` file sets `Options=isolate=strict`, netavark's strict isolation, so a container on another network can't reach this one's containers at all, and they can't reach any other network's.
  - **Why:** before this, one app's container reached another service's container directly, from its network's gateway (found on a VM while connecting Navidrome to single sign-on, §5.2). A compromised app could then go past Caddy, the WAF and a single sign-on wall to any other service, and send headers that only Caddy should send. The only ways in are now the ones meant for it: Caddy, through the routes' ports published on 127.0.0.1 (§6.5); a service's `ports:` on the server's address; and the lab's own addresses, which go through Caddy like anyone's.
  - **What still works (checked on a VM, docs/ASSUMPTIONS.md C14):** a service's own containers reaching each other by name and their databases; Caddy reaching the published ports; containers reaching the sign-in page through `host-gateway` (§5.2) and the internet through pasta. Nothing else crossed networks: Caddy and CrowdSec run on pasta (CrowdSec on rootless Podman's default, which is pasta), not on a service network.
  - **Strict, not `isolate=true`:** `true` only keeps isolated networks apart, so a container on a network without it (Podman's default network, or one made by hand as the roostack user) still reached every service.
  - **Existing labs:** Podman doesn't change a network that already exists when its file changes (Quadlet's `podman network create --ignore`, ASSUMPTIONS C8), so a lab installed before this keeps open networks (removing a service doesn't delete its network either, C9) until each is removed by hand (`podman network rm`, with the service stopped) or the lab is installed fresh. Only fresh installs are supported before release, so the reconciler doesn't recreate them. *Considered:* the reconciler removing a network whose file changed (stopping the service's containers first): worth doing if a network's options change again after release.
  - *Considered:* one pod per service (its containers share one network namespace), which isolates as well but changes how every package's containers find each other and publish ports; and firewall rules of Roostack's own inside the rootless network namespace (netavark manages that firewall and sets it up again whenever a network starts, so it would be a second set of rules to keep in step).

### 6.5 Reverse Proxy & TLS — Locked

- **Proxy: Caddy**, run as a rootless container from a Roostack-built image that includes the DNS provider plugins.
- **DNS providers — Locked (2026-09-26):** Cloudflare, Amazon Route 53, Porkbun, Gandi, GoDaddy, Namecheap and Spaceship, defined in one registry (`internal/dns`). Each provider's credentials are stored as Podman secrets and passed to Caddy as environment variables, so they never appear in the Caddyfile. Before saving, the wizard checks credentials by **creating and deleting a temporary TXT record** through the provider's public HTTP API (Namecheap: a read-only check, since its API can only rewrite whole zones). That tests what certificate issuance needs.
- **Dependencies for provider APIs:** prefer plain HTTP calls or small generic libraries; avoid large vendor SDKs (e.g. the AWS SDK). Roostack's checks use the standard library only (including AWS SigV4 signing for Route 53).
- Roostack generates a readable Caddyfile from desired state and reloads Caddy (same pattern as Quadlet generation).
- **Not user-selectable.** One proxy keeps testing and config generation manageable. Users can add custom Caddy snippets, globally and per service.
- **Rootless on 80/443:** `roostack-agent` lowers `net.ipv4.ip_unprivileged_port_start` so the proxy needs no root.
- **Proxy networking (Option C) — Locked (2026-09-26):**
  - Caddy runs in its **own pasta network namespace** (`Network=pasta:--map-host-loopback,169.254.1.3`), publishing 80/443. Pasta keeps each visitor's **real IP**, which the private-route check and CrowdSec rely on.
  - Each routed container stays on its service's private network and is **published on `127.0.0.1:<port>`** only. Caddy reaches it at `169.254.1.3:<port>`, which pasta maps to the host's loopback. The Roostack UI listens on `127.0.0.1:8480` and is reached the same way.
  - Ports come from a central range (20000–29999), are allocated once, and are stored in the instance file (`routes.<name>.port`) so they never change.
  - **Why:** on the Debian 13 VM, rootless bridge networks replaced client IPs with `10.89.0.x` (Podman's `rootlessport`), which Caddy would treat as private, exposing "private" services to the internet. Rootless Podman can't put one container on both pasta/host networking and a bridge network, and the host side can't reach bridge addresses.
  - **Rejected:** Caddy on host networking (works, but shares the host's network namespace); a host-network forwarder using the PROXY protocol in front of a bridge-network Caddy (two proxies, no HTTP/3).
- **TLS: Let's Encrypt via DNS-01.** This is required so that private (LAN/VPN-only) services get valid certificates without being reachable from the internet.
- **Wildcard certificate by default** (e.g. `*.lab.example.com`), with services on subdomains sharing it. Rationale:
  - One issuance and renewal instead of one per service, so fewer rate-limit issues.
  - Subdomain names don't appear in public Certificate Transparency logs, so the list of services you run isn't published.
  - DNS-01 is already required, so there's no extra setup.
  - Trade-off: one certificate covers all services. That's acceptable on a single-owner host.
- **Rationale for Caddy:** best automatic HTTPS, readable config, easy custom snippets. **Considered:** Traefik (many DNS providers built in, but verbose config tied to container labels); Nginx + certbot (familiar, but TLS is a separate tool and config is verbose).

### 6.5.1 DNS Records & Local Access — Locked (2026-09-26)

Roostack manages the DNS records its services need, through the same DNS provider credentials used for certificates.

- **Private services (default):** one **wildcard record** (`*.<domain>`) pointing at the lab's **LAN IP**, in public DNS. Devices at home reach services without local DNS. Anyone can look the record up, but only the home network can reach the address, and service names stay out of public DNS (like the wildcard certificate).
- **Router DNS rebinding protection:** some routers drop DNS answers containing private IPs. Roostack **detects** this by comparing the home network's resolver with a public resolver, and shows instructions (allow the domain in the router, or use local DNS).
- **Local DNS** (e.g. Pi-hole) is optional, for later. Getting every device to use it means changing the router's DHCP DNS setting (router-specific, sometimes impossible). Roostack running DHCP itself was **rejected**: if the lab dies, the home network stops working.
- **Public services (stage 2):** explicit records pointing at the **public IP**; explicit records override the wildcard. Devices at home reach them via the router's NAT loopback (Roostack can test this).
- **Dynamic public IPs (stage 2):** Roostack updates the records itself (§6.11, 2026-09-30; ddns-updater was the earlier plan).
- **Cloudflare proxy (stage 3):** offered per public service, **off by default**, with advantages and disadvantages listed in the UI:
  - For: hides the home IP; DDoS protection; works around some ISP port blocking.
  - Against: Cloudflare decrypts the traffic (at odds with "own your data"); free plan upload limit of 100 MB per request; Cloudflare's terms restrict large media streaming on the free plan.
  - When proxied, Caddy trusts `CF-Connecting-IP` **only from Cloudflare's published IP ranges**, so the private check and CrowdSec still see real visitors.
  - Packages declare proxy warnings, e.g. large uploads (Immich, until its chunked uploads land) or media streaming (Plex, Jellyfin, stricter). The format is to be designed.
- **Ownership:** Roostack only changes records it created (tracked in its state, plus a comment where the provider supports it). An existing record with the same name that Roostack didn't create is never overwritten automatically; the UI explains the conflict. A record that already has the right value is adopted.
- **Deleting — as built (2026-10-19):** a record Roostack no longer needs (a public address made private, an old address's records when it ends, §6.6) is deleted only if it's Roostack's (its state) and still as Roostack left it: read first, deleted only when it holds exactly the value Roostack set. One changed by hand since (another value, or values added) is the Owner's now: left in place, and no longer Roostack's, with a line in the log. A delete that fails (the provider refused or didn't answer) keeps the record Roostack's, so the next sync or check tries again; a restart part-way through carries on from what's left, since each record stops being Roostack's only once it's gone. Roostack Cloud's records API can't delete (a roost.ac name's records go when the name is released), so there a record no longer needed points home, as before. Considered: deleting whatever is at the name (would delete the Owner's records); keeping a changed record Roostack's (it would be overwritten if the name were wanted again).
- **Taking over a record:** the Owner can explicitly take over a conflicting record from the dashboard. A confirmation page shows the current and new values, warns that anything relying on the current value stops working, and needs an "I understand" tick. The takeover is recorded in the audit log.
- **Switching management on or off** is done from the dashboard's "Home network access" card (Owner only), with the Namecheap risk opt-in where relevant, and applies without a restart. Turning it off leaves records in place.
- **Namecheap:** its API can only replace a domain's whole record set, so record management there is **off unless the Owner ticks an opt-in** with a clear warning, and strongly recommends another provider (e.g. Cloudflare).
- **No usable public IP (CGNAT):** public exposure needs a relay. Future options: Cloudflare Tunnel, or a Roostack Cloud relay (§4).
- **Stages:** 1) wildcard to LAN IP + rebinding check; 2) public records + dynamic DNS (with public exposure, firewall, CrowdSec); 3) Cloudflare proxy.

### 6.6 A lab's address (domains) — Locked, changed 2026-10-06

- **Changed (2026-10-06): an own domain is optional.** A lab gets its addresses from either **its own domain** (with a supported DNS provider, API token for DNS-01, as today) or **a free `roost.ac` name** from Roostack Cloud (e.g. `smiths.roost.ac`, with services at `photos.smiths.roost.ac`), whose DNS and certificate challenges Cloud handles for the lab. A lab can switch between them later. Getting a name means linking the lab to Cloud (a free account); nothing else about Cloud is needed.
- **Before (2026-09-26 to 2026-10-06):** v1 required a user-owned domain, set up once at install, with `roost.ac` names planned for later. Built that way: the package installs a placeholder domain, and until a domain is set up nothing gets an HTTPS certificate. Untangling this is a task (TASKS, "Domain optional").
- **Certificates for an own domain — Locked (2026-10-06):** Roostack picks the best way available, automatically:
  1. **A supported DNS provider:** DNS-01 through the provider's API, one wildcard certificate (as today).
  2. **Benched (user, 2026-10-10): an unsupported provider.** Options 2 and 3 below were locked on 2026-10-06 and are kept as the design, but aren't being built now. The people they serve are few: they must be able to add CNAME records by hand, yet be unable to use any of the seven supported providers, when moving a domain's DNS hosting to one (Cloudflare is free) keeps the domain where it's registered. Meanwhile the help page says to do that. When this is built, both come together, and **reaching the lab on port 443 is a first-class choice** in the address step, not a hidden fallback.
     - **Through Roostack Cloud (lab linked):** DNS-01 by challenge delegation. Let's Encrypt wants a TXT record with a new value at every renewal (every 60 days, sooner as certificates get shorter), which nobody can keep up by hand; a CNAME added once (`_acme-challenge.<domain>` → a name Cloud gives the lab) moves that record to where the lab can write it, the mechanism a roost.ac name already uses. Still one wildcard; Cloud only touches that challenge name and never holds the certificate or its key. **Refined (2026-10-10):** the addresses should be a CNAME too (`*.<domain>` → a name under Cloud the lab keeps pointed at the right address, as for a roost.ac name), so the owner adds two records once and never touches DNS again, even when the server's or the home's address changes; otherwise they'd also keep A records up to date by hand. Needs a provider that allows a wildcard CNAME (nearly all); the bare domain can't be one, and isn't needed (Roostack is at `roost.<domain>`). A side benefit: Roostack holds no key to the domain at all, where most providers' API keys can rewrite the whole domain or account.
     - **Without Cloud, the lab reachable on port 443** (a VPS, port forwarding, or a relay): TLS-ALPN-01, a certificate per name. Let's Encrypt only issues wildcards after a DNS check, so each service gets its own certificate, and the names appear in public certificate logs (a wildcard hides them). Services only on the home network can't get a certificate this way.
- **Three states — Locked (2026-10-06), agreed from a mock-up:**
  - **No address yet:** a real state, not a placeholder domain. Services install and run but have no web address: each shows "Waiting for an address" with a link to give the lab one, and its Details give technical users the local port and an SSH tunnel to reach it meanwhile. **Until then, a service's own address is its local one** (`http://localhost:<port>`, host `localhost`), where a tunnel reaches it: apps that must know their address (Vaultwarden's `DOMAIN`, Forgejo's root URL) refuse to start without one (found on a fresh install, 2026-10-06). Packages use the route's `.URL` for it. One item on the home page's attention list. Caddy, DNS records and public access only act once there's an address. The UI is reached over the SSH tunnel (§6.3.2), as without a domain today.
  - **A roost.ac name:** services at `photos.smiths.roost.ac`; **Roostack itself at the name** (`smiths.roost.ac`), not `roost.smiths.roost.ac`.
  - **An own domain:** as before, Roostack at `roost.<domain>`.
- **Choosing it — Locked (2026-10-06):** the wizard's step 3 is **"Your lab's address"** (was "Your domain"), and Settings → Domain and certificates becomes **Your lab's address**. Two choices: **a free roost.ac address** (recommended: type a name, see whether it's free, "Continue to Roostack Cloud" takes the same tab there to sign up or log in, Cloud sends it back, and the lab finishes by itself) or **your own domain** (then the domain, an email address and who runs its DNS, as before), with a link to a help page on buying and managing a domain (which registrars' DNS Roostack supports, and how). **"Set this up later"** stays, saying services can't be reached until the lab has an address. The roost.ac option is always offered (Roostack Cloud is meant to be permanent), unless Cloud is turned off (below).
- **Switching — Locked (2026-10-06):** from a roost.ac name to an own domain, back, or to another name: every service's address changes, so **the old addresses keep working for 30 days** (Caddy serves both names; the old certificates kept), with a notice listing what to update (bookmarks, phone apps such as Bitwarden's or Immich's server address). Then they stop.
  - **As built (2026-10-07), for own domains** (roost.ac names come with Cloud's DNS): saving a different domain keeps the one being left in `lab.yaml` (`old_address`: the domain, its DNS provider settings, and when it stops), and copies its provider's credentials to their own Podman secrets (`roostack-caddy-dns-old-<field>`) before the new ones replace them. Caddy then has a second wildcard site for the old domain, with its own certificate (it can still renew, through the old provider), and every route answers to both names (`host photos.new photos.old`), with the same protections. **The old addresses work exactly as before, not as redirects** (agreed 2026-10-07): many phone apps don't follow a redirect to another host. Apps' own settings that hold the address (Vaultwarden's `DOMAIN`) use the new one at once. Home shows everyone a notice until then (a warning in the last week); the Owner's links to Settings → Your lab's address, which lists every old → new address (Roostack's too), says passkeys made for the old address don't move, and offers **Stop the old addresses now**. Each service's page shows its old address and until when. An hourly check ends it: `lab.yaml` first and a restart, and the old credentials are deleted only after that (Podman deletes a secret a running container uses, which then can't restart: ASSUMPTIONS C12). Only one old address is kept: switching again within 30 days stops the earlier one at once (the change form says so). *Until 2026-10-19 Roostack stopped updating the old domain's DNS records and left them behind afterwards (VM3's test of lab.roostack.cloud left three, 2026-10-14); now, below.* Considered: redirects (break apps), keeping several old addresses (more secrets and certificates for a rare case).
  - **The old domain's records — as built (2026-10-19):** while the old address works, the records manager (`dnsrecords`, `old.go`) keeps the records Roostack set under it up to date with the old address's own login (its `dns.OldSecretName` secrets), at each sync as the current domain's: the wildcard (and a roost.ac name itself) where the current wildcard points; a public address's record at the way in's address while the same service is public at the new address, and deleted once it's private (§6.5.1 "Deleting"); CNAMEs to the relay and the proof's TXT left as they are (they don't follow the home). Nothing new is created under an old address. When it ends (the hourly check, or **Stop the old addresses now**, which now sets its end to now and finishes it straight away), its records are deleted with that login **before** the login goes: `lab.yaml` keeps the ended `old_address` (no longer served: Caddy, the routes and the pages all ask `Lab.Old`, which needs its end in the future) until they're gone, then it goes, and its secrets with it after Caddy no longer names them. If the old provider refuses (the old login deleted) or doesn't answer, the log and **Settings → Your lab's address** say so plainly, with the provider's answer and the records still there, and Home has a warning; Roostack keeps the login and tries again every hour, and the Owner can delete them by hand and choose **I've deleted them**, which forgets them and finishes the old address. Switching again within the 30 days deletes the earlier old address's records first (switching back to it doesn't). Records Roostack still holds under no address the lab has (out of the old login's zone, or left by an earlier switch that couldn't delete) are listed on the same page to delete by hand. A roost.ac name's records aren't deleted by the lab: Cloud removes them when the lab releases the name. Where the Owner had turned record management off for the old domain, its records are left and forgotten. Where one domain is under the other (`lab.example.com` → `home.lab.example.com`), a record belongs to the longer one. Tested against every provider's fake (`internal/dns/dnstest`): a login refused, a provider failing after one delete, a restart part-way through, records changed by hand; not yet with a real account. Considered: one try at the end, then a list to delete by hand (a provider down for an hour would leave them all); giving up after some days (the login would go while records are left, with nothing said); deleting with the new domain's login (often another account or provider).
  - **Records of addresses the lab no longer has — as built (2026-10-19):** a dev VM (VM3) listed 17 records from seven earlier addresses as "left under an old address", 14 of them roost.ac names' (the test Cloud's `roost.test`, `test.roost.ac` and `roostack.dev` names) that the Owner has no login to delete. They piled up because **losing a roost.ac name** (unlinking, or Cloud no longer knowing the lab: `setup.DropRoostAc`) took the address away without letting go of its records, so every later address listed them; the other three are an own domain's at Cloudflare, left on 2026-10-14 when the VM went back to an earlier roost.ac name with no switch in its log (most likely `lab.yaml` put back by hand during tests), and are listed rightly. Now each record Roostack sets also notes the provider it's at (a `dns.via.<name>/<type>` row beside `dns.owned.`, cleared with it). **A roost.ac name's records are the lab's while it has the name** (current or old address: the records manager keeps them pointing at the right place, and its own record of them is what lets it point a public address home again), **and are let go as soon as it doesn't**, whichever way the name went: at each sync, records set through Cloud under no address the lab has are forgotten (logged), never listed. Cloud removes a name's records when it's released, and keeps them only while it keeps a name for the lab (§6.6 "Switching names keeps the old one"). Records at the Owner's own provider are listed as before, under **the provider they're at** ("Delete them at Cloudflare"; Home says the same when there's one). Records set before the provider was noted (pre-release labs only) are listed with a sentence covering both cases ("If that address was a roost.ac name, there's nothing to do there…"), with the same **I've deleted them**. The other ways records outlive an address were checked: switching again within 30 days deletes the earlier old address's records first, and if that fails (the provider down for the two minutes it gets), its login is replaced and they're listed, naming the provider (unchanged: SPEC accepted it above); a full-lab restore brings back `lab.yaml` and the database together, so its records match its addresses; unlinking (and Cloud forgetting the lab) is the case above; ending the relay removes the proof's TXT record and the CNAMEs at the next sync, under the current address. While a lab has no address and no old one, the records manager doesn't run, so a dropped name's records are let go once it has an address again (nothing is listed meanwhile). Considered: not recording a roost.ac name's records at all (the old name's and public addresses' records couldn't then be pointed home); letting go of them in `DropRoostAc` itself (setup doesn't hold the records, and the sync covers every way a name goes, including hand edits); guessing Cloud's records from the name's suffix (it comes from Cloud, and has changed: `roost.test`, `roostack.dev`, `test.roost.ac`).
- **A roost.ac name alongside an own domain — Leaning (2026-10-06):** the lab's addresses are a **primary** (used for every service by default) and, later, **extra** ones: e.g. an own domain as primary, plus a roost.ac name for one service shared through the relay without touching the domain's DNS, alongside custom domains (§6.11). The records are designed for several addresses from the start; the extra addresses come after the single-address version.
- **Turning Roostack Cloud off — Locked (2026-10-06):** `cloud: { disabled: true }` in `lab.yaml` turns off every Roostack Cloud feature: nothing is offered (the roost.ac choice, Cloud backups, checks from outside) and Roostack never contacts Cloud's API. It's deliberately only in the file, not in Settings: for people who want Roostack fully self-contained. The service registry (`registry.disabled`, §8.3.3) and Roostack's apt repository are other services on roostack.cloud, which this doesn't turn off (noted 2026-10-19, for the help's "Your data and your keys").
- **Linking a lab to Roostack Cloud — Locked (2026-10-07), agreed from a mock-up.** Everything Cloud does for a lab (a roost.ac name, later backups and checks from outside) starts with linking it to a Cloud account, from the lab. The lab's side:
  - **Only the controller talks to Cloud.** It makes a key pair (ECDSA P-256) whose private key never leaves the server, and asks Cloud for a **link code**, sending the lab's name, the roost.ac name it wants (if any), Roostack's version and a certificate request for that key. Nothing else: no email address, no list of services. Because the key goes with the first request, someone who sees the code can't swap in a key of their own. Cloud answers with the code, the page to open, and a secret only the lab holds, used to ask about it.
  - **The Owner confirms in Cloud — same tab, as built (2026-10-10).** The button posts, and Roostack sends that tab on to Cloud's page with the code in it (a short "Going to Roostack Cloud…" page with a meta refresh). There they sign up or log in, check the code matches the one the lab shows, and confirm ("Link and go back to Test lab"). The link request carries a **return address**: the lab's own waiting page, built from the address the Owner's browser used (the form's `Origin` header), so it works however the lab is reached. Cloud sends the browser back there, and the page shows the steps (linked, address claimed) and **moves on by itself**: in the wizard to the next step (with a note saying the lab's address), in Settings to Roostack Cloud; if the link failed, back to the address step to try again. Meanwhile the lab asks Cloud every few seconds (no incoming connection needed), so nothing secret passes through the browser; Settings still shows the code and its expiry (10 minutes) for anyone who comes back another way. Cancelling just forgets it. Considered: a new window (the first build), dropped because the desktop app's browser turned its form post into a GET, and the leftover tab plus "Continue setup" confused people (user, 2026-10-07).
  - **Once confirmed,** the lab gets its certificate and uses it for every request to Cloud from then on (mutual TLS, as between a lab's servers). It renews itself (a new key each time) once two thirds of its life has passed, and the lab checks in once a day (and when Roostack starts): "last contact" on the page.
  - **Never magical:** Settings → **Roostack Cloud** (hidden when Cloud is turned off) shows the account, since when, what Cloud is used for, the last contact, and under Details the certificate (fingerprint, expiry), where its key is (`<state>/cloud/`, 0600), Cloud's address, and a log of the lab's recent requests to Cloud.
    - **The request log shows what was sent — as built (2026-10-19):** `<state>/cloud/requests.log`, one JSON object a line, the latest 200: when, method and path (or the challenge server's address, for the test write when a name is claimed), each request's JSON body as `Sent`, and Cloud's status or why it wasn't reached; the same request with the same answer adds to one line. Never the answers (storage keys come back in them). Secrets are left out by an allowlist (`cloud.recordedFields`): a field shows only if it's listed as safe, any other shows "(not recorded)" (the link's secret, the recovery identity's signatures), and a test fails while any field of a request type in `cloudapi` is undecided, so a new field can't slip in unseen. Long values (a certificate request, a list of bans) are shortened. Settings → Roostack Cloud shows it under "Recent requests to Roostack Cloud", linking to the help's "Your data and your keys", which says what each request is for. Considered: logging bodies with known secret fields removed (a field added later would be logged until someone noticed); logging only field names (doesn't show *what* was said, which is the point). Not in it: what Caddy itself writes to Cloud's challenge server when it renews a certificate, and visitors' traffic through the relay (Caddy's log, and WireGuard).
      - **Added (2026-10-19):** a roost.ac name's records, as the records manager changes them through Cloud's records API (`cloud.Service.RecordsTransport` on its HTTP client: requests carrying Cloud's login header that change something, with the whole address and `RecordValue`'s `value`, the address the record points at, which public DNS shows anyway). Reads aren't recorded: they send only the record's name, and the manager reads each record every 5 minutes while anything is public, which would push the rest out of the 200 lines. And a fresh install's restore from Cloud (`Service.RestoreAccess`, through the link's client though not linked), kept through the restore, which puts back the lab's own Cloud folder: `KeepRequests` reads the install's log before and adds it to the restored one after. Caddy's own writes at each renewal are still not in it: Caddy talks to the challenge server itself, and its journal is the system journal, which the sandboxed daemon can't read (docs/ASSUMPTIONS.md A10). Recording them needs a choice (TASKS): Caddy writing its `tls` loggers to a file the daemon reads (what happened, not the request itself), or Caddy's `server_url` pointing at a local proxy in the daemon that records and forwards (the exact request, but renewals then depend on the daemon).
  - **What the lab and Cloud tell each other each day — as built (2026-10-18):** after each check-in the lab sends Cloud a report (`PUT /v1/lab/report`, `cloudapi.LabReport`): its name as it is now (since 2026-10-18, below), the addresses of the people who get its **Problems** emails (§6.13; never Members, who can't get that group: the user agreed, 2026-10-18), and each server backing up to Roostack Cloud with its last backup there (its main backup or a copy, by its folder's name, the server's ID, with the server's name beside it). Nothing else. Cloud's emails about the lab (a name approved or refused, a suspension, the lab gone quiet, its recovery key used) then go to those people too, **once each has confirmed** from a link Cloud emails it (changed 2026-10-18, the user: unconfirmed at first; Cloud keeps 10 per lab, and says in the check-in how each stands, `LabInfo.Recipients`: shown since 2026-10-18 under each person on Settings → Email and as "Emails to" on Settings → Roostack Cloud, and asked again straight after a report that changed the addresses, so a new one shows as waiting the same day rather than the next), each saying why they're getting it and with a link to stop (Cloud's DESIGN §3b, "Who gets Cloud's messages"); Cloud's page for the lab shows each server's last backup. Cloud's answers say what it holds back: the check-in (`LabInfo`) says why the account is suspended and which names wait for approval, were refused or are suspended (with why); the relay's answers say the same, and whether the account turned the relay off in Cloud or Cloud suspended it (`Relay.TurnedOff`, `Relay.Suspended`); a claim says at once when its name waits. Settings → Roostack Cloud ("Names on hold", the suspension) and Needs attention show them (refused and suspended names are problems; a waiting one is information), and the lab checks in hourly instead of daily while Cloud has something on hold, so a decision or a lift shows soon. **Turned off in Cloud** (the account's page can turn the relay off, even with the lab off): the lab's own updates still leave it off, and Public access says when it was turned off, with **Turn the relay on** (`RelayRequest.TurnOn`; before, the Owner turned the relay off and on again, which makes a new relay).
