Details on the Cloud page says when the lab last reported, and what. **Either side older:** every field is optional and missing means unknown. An older Cloud answers the report 404 (the lab carries on quietly) and says nothing held back, so the lab shows what it did before (requests refused with Cloud's message); an older lab never reports (Cloud's page shows no backup times and emails only the Owner) and isn't told what's held back (as before). Considered: asking each person before sending their address (they chose to get the lab's problems by email, and the help and Details say where it goes); the lab asking Cloud whenever a page loads (slow pages, and nothing to show when Cloud is down); a separate "notifications" list for Cloud (one more setting to keep in step). **The lab's name (2026-10-18):** Cloud showed the name the lab gave when it linked, forever: a lab renamed in Settings → Lab name kept its old name in Cloud's portal, console and emails, and a lab linked from the setup wizard was "My lab" there (and in its certificate's CommonName), since Roostack read its name once at start and the wizard names the lab without restarting. Now the link, the report, the network's join codes and CA, and what other labs' guests are told read the name from `lab.yaml` when they need it (`config.NameAt`); the report carries it (`LabReport.Name`, left out if Cloud wouldn't take it: over 80 characters, e.g. a long domain standing in for a lab with no name), and Cloud renames the lab when it differs. An older Cloud ignores it.
  - **Unlinking** from the lab asks for confirmation, naming what stops working (a roost.ac address, and with it every service's address: the lab goes back to "no address yet"), tells Cloud, and deletes the key. If Cloud can't be reached it unlinks anyway and says so: the account page can remove the lab too.
  - **Unlinked from Cloud's side, and restores — Locked (2026-10-07):** the link is in Roostack's state, so it's backed up and a restored lab is still linked. If the lab was unlinked meanwhile (from the account, from the lab before an older backup was restored, or the certificate expired while the lab was off), Cloud refuses the certificate at the next contact; the lab then forgets the link and "needs attention" says it's no longer linked and offers to link it again. Cloud being unreachable is different: the link is kept and the page says since when it couldn't be reached.
  - `cloud: { url: …, ca_file: … }` in `lab.yaml` points a lab at a test Cloud (`ca_file` trusts a test certificate authority); `disabled: true` still turns all of it off.
  - **For development,** Roostack's tests use a fake of the API (`internal/cloud/cloudtest`), and a dev VM links to a development Cloud run from Cloud's repository (since 2026-10-07; `cmd/roostack-fakecloud` did it until the real endpoints existed). The contract both sides import is the public package `cloudapi`. Roostack's default Cloud address is its API, `https://api.roostack.cloud`; people confirm links on its website, `cloud.roostack.cloud`. **Changed (2026-10-17):** it was `https://api.roost.ac` with the website on `roost.ac`; Cloud's own names moved to roostack.cloud, so roost.ac holds only labs' names (a lab's name can't then be mistaken for Cloud's pages, nor its cookies reach them). Nothing was released, so no lab needs moving: one that set `cloud.url` keeps it, and one that didn't now talks to the new address.
  - **Not yet:** the recovery key's proof for account recovery (Cloud's design: its cryptography first).
- **A roost.ac name as the lab's address — Locked (2026-10-07), as built:** a linked lab claims its name from Cloud (with its certificate) and gets back the full name and **an acme-dns account** that can only write that name's records. Roostack treats Cloud as one more DNS provider (`roostack-cloud`, never offered in the list): the account's fields are its Podman secrets like any provider's, so the rest is unchanged.
  - **Certificates:** Caddy's `caddy-dns/acmedns` module (added to Roostack's Caddy build, chosen 2026-10-07) writes the challenge record through Cloud's acme-dns API, for one wildcard certificate covering the name too. The module reads placeholders only in its password, so the account's other fields (none secret) are kept in `lab.yaml` (`dns.values`) and written into Caddy's config as they are; only the password is a Podman secret (ASSUMPTIONS C13). **Tried for real (2026-10-07):** VM3, linked to a development Cloud publishing to roost.ac's real DNS, got Let's Encrypt certificates for `smiths.test.roost.ac` and its wildcard, both served over HTTPS and verified. Considered: our own Caddy module using the lab's certificate (no extra credential, but our code inside Caddy, and Caddy would need the lab's key). The account can already get certificates for the name, so letting it also set the name's A records (Cloud's records API, same account) adds no power.
  - **Records:** as for an own domain, Roostack manages them: `*.name` and the name itself to the server's home-network address (Roostack's pages are at the name), public services' own records to the home's public address. Cloud allows nothing outside the lab's name, and no public wildcard.
  - **Getting it:** in the address step, **Continue to Roostack Cloud** links the lab (same tab, back again afterwards) and then claims the name by itself; once linked, **Use this address** claims it at once. The lab then restarts with it, the address step done. A previous address keeps working for 30 days, as when switching domains.
  - **A held name** (kept for the account that had it, after its lab was unlinked) isn't refused before linking: until then Cloud can't know whose account is asking, so it says the name is held, Roostack says "if that's yours, you'll get it back once the lab is linked to it", and the claim, once linked, decides (found by trying it, 2026-10-07: unlinking and linking again lost the lab its own name).
  - **Losing it:** unlinking, or Cloud no longer knowing the lab, takes the address away: the lab goes back to "no address yet" (the name stays held for the account for a year, Cloud's side); its records go at Cloud, and the lab lets go of its own note of them ("Records of addresses the lab no longer has", above, 2026-10-19).
  - **Checked when claimed (2026-10-11):** before the lab uses a newly claimed name, it writes a test value through Cloud's challenge API with the new login and waits (up to 45 seconds) to see it at `_acme-challenge.<name>` on the zone's own name servers (not for `.test` names, never in public DNS). A failure says what's wrong and leaves the lab as it was. Without it, a cached certificate would hide broken credentials until the next renewal, two months later. Tried on VM3 against the test Cloud: the claim took 11 seconds, and the certificates for the new name followed.
  - **Switching names keeps the old one — as built (2026-10-19):** claiming a new name released the lab's old one at Cloud in the same step (its records and challenge login gone), so a claim that then failed (the check above, or using it) left the lab on a released name, and the old name's 30 days of old addresses (above) didn't work: Cloud had already removed its records. Now a lab whose address is a roost.ac name claims with `NameRequest.KeepEarlier`: Cloud releases the earlier name as before (held for the account, no longer the lab's name in use) but keeps its records published and its login working for `cloudapi.KeptFor` (31 days), and says so (`NameClaim.Kept`). If the new name fails, the lab gives it back (`POST /v1/lab/name/release` with the new name): Cloud releases it and the kept name is the lab's in use again, its records and login as they were, so the lab carries on as if nothing happened. If it works, the old name is the lab's old address, and the lab releases it (the same call, with the old name) at the first check-in after that ends (30 days, or **Stop the old addresses now**; both restart Roostack, which checks in at start); a give-back Cloud didn't hear is retried at each check-in too (`link.json`'s `kept`, `give_back`). Cloud ends a kept name by itself after its 31 days, so a lab that never releases it can't hold names. One is kept at most: claiming again ends the one before, as the lab keeps one old address. A kept name isn't relayed (only the name in use is). **Either side older:** an older lab never sends `KeepEarlier`, and Cloud releases its earlier name with the claim exactly as before; an older Cloud ignores it (no `Kept` in its answer), and the lab doesn't call release. Considered: the lab claiming its old name back after a failure (it's held for the account) — new credentials, a restart, and minutes without records; a two-step claim (reserve, then confirm) — more state at Cloud for the same result.
  - **The suffix comes from Cloud (2026-10-11):** names end in whatever the lab's Cloud says (`GET /v1/info`, asked once a day): roost.ac, a test Cloud's roostack.dev, the fake's roost.test. The pages show it wherever they named roost.ac; until a Cloud has answered they say roost.ac.
  - **Trying it:** the fake Cloud gives names under `roost.test`, so a dev VM goes through everything except Let's Encrypt actually issuing a certificate (it refuses `.test`).
- **Open:** a lab with neither (no domain, not linked to Cloud) beyond "no address yet". Options: stay as it is (services wait for an address), or HTTPS by IP address (§6.3.2, later).
- **Rejected for v1:** a local CA (a root certificate on every device is bad UX); plain HTTP (browser warnings, and some clients such as Bitwarden require HTTPS).

### 6.7 Storage Layout — Locked (defaults may change before launch)

Default paths:

```
/var/lib/roostack/                 # Roostack's own state: desired state, secrets, users
/srv/roostack/services/<service>/  # per-service data (bind mounts)
    data/
    db/
/srv/roostack/media/               # large shared media; can be a separate disk
```

- **Paths are configurable**, and all defaults are defined in **one central place** in the code so they can be changed easily. No migration support is needed before launch; the defaults will be locked before release.
- **Bind mounts, not Podman named volumes.** Data sits in a visible, predictable location (Principle 4), and it's easy to back up and move (Principle 1).
- The media location can be configured separately, e.g. a small system SSD plus a large data drive.
- **Open / to prototype early:** rootless user ID mapping. Files owned by UIDs inside containers appear as mapped IDs (e.g. 100000+) on the host. We need to settle how this affects browsing, backups, and media shared between services (e.g. Jellyfin and Immich), and where Podman's `keep-id` option helps.
- **Disk space — as built (2026-09-28):** each server reports the disk holding its Roostack data (`statfs` of `/srv/roostack`: total, used, free to the roostack user, and what the filesystem keeps back), with what Roostack's parts take: its services' data, the backups it keeps for the lab's servers, and those it keeps for other labs. Free and used space come from `statfs` alone (instant); only the breakdown is counted, with `du -s -B1` (space on the disk, as `df` counts it) at the lowest CPU and disk priority (`nice -n 19 ionice -c3`). `du` reads every file's metadata: quick on an SSD (300,000 files: under a second), but a large photo library on a spinning disk can take a minute or more and fills the kernel's metadata cache. So sizes are counted **once a day, right after the nightly backup**, while restic's reads of the same metadata are still cached, and otherwise only when a size is missing or over a day old (no backups set up, a new service); pages show the last count and when it was made, and never wait for one; while a count runs, the disk block says "counting…" and refreshes itself until it's done (2026-10-03: it said "not counted yet" until the page was reloaded). Settings → Servers shows it for each server, with a bar coloured by part (2026-10-03: services' data, the lab's backups, other labs' backups, the system; then the space promised to other labs and not used yet, striped; then free; and at the far end what the filesystem reserves for the system, striped grey), each part named with its size in a key underneath, and on the server storing other labs' backups, the space promised to them (their quotas) and how much of it they don't use yet. **Low space** is a warning below 5 GB or 10% free, and a problem below 2 GB or 5% (since 2026-10-17, on a disk under 20 GB the 5 GB and 2 GB become a quarter and a tenth of the disk: a 2 GB USB drive with 1.9 GB free was "running out of disk space"), shown on the home page and on Servers; so is having promised other labs more space than is free, and (2026-10-03) earlier, a warning once the space they could still add reaches into the 10% of the disk kept free for the lab's own services and backups. The backup plan shows the free space where each backup is (this server's drives, or another server's disk) and warns when it's nearly full. **Every disk — as built (2026-10-03):** each server reports every disk holding a folder Roostack uses: the system's (`/`), a separate disk for services' data or the backup store, a separate media disk, and each backup drive (the repository folder on it). Folders on the same disk (same device) count once; a disk is named by the most important folder on it (System disk, Data disk, Media disk, or a backup drive's name as the backup plan gives it, such as USB drive BACKUP; until 2026-10-17 "Backup drive <name>", which read "backup drive USB drive BACKUP") and shows where it's mounted when a server has more than one. Each disk's parts are what's on it: services' data split by folder (a service's `services` and `media` folders can be on different disks), the backup store's two parts, and a drive's backups; on a drive the rest is "Everything else". Low space, and the space promised to other labs, are per disk (promised space on the disk holding the backup store), and warnings name the disk when there's more than one ("This server's USB drive BACKUP is running out of disk space"). An unplugged drive isn't listed. Considered: listing every mounted filesystem (most mean nothing to Roostack's user, such as /boot/efi).

### 6.8 Databases — Locked

- **One database container per service** (e.g. Immich gets its own Postgres), not a shared instance.
- Roostack **standardises the pattern**: every package uses the same Roostack-managed Postgres/MySQL/MariaDB/Redis setup, with the same data location (`<service>/db/`), backup method, health checks and upgrade process.
- **Rationale:**
  - Each app runs the exact database version and extensions it's tested with (e.g. Immich needs specific Postgres versions and a vector extension).
  - Upgrades and failures are isolated to one service.
  - Each service is self-contained, so moving it to another host or Roostack Cloud means moving one folder plus its definition (§4 portability).
  - The cost, roughly 30–50 MB RAM per idle instance, is acceptable on NUC-class hardware.
- **Rejected:** a single shared Postgres. It saves RAM, but it couples versions and upgrades, makes it a single point of failure, and makes backing up or moving one service harder.

### 6.9 Backups — Locked

**Tool: restic**, called as a CLI (`--json` output) behind a small internal interface (backup, restore, list snapshots, prune, check), so the tool can be swapped later.

- **Rationale:**
  - A Go single binary; encrypted and deduplicated, with many backends (local, SFTP, S3, B2, rclone, REST server).
  - Users can recover independently with the plain `restic` CLI and the recovery key, even if Roostack is broken. There's a large community and many docs (Principles 1 and 4).
  - Roostack owns scheduling and retention as part of desired state, so there's no competing policy engine.
  - The append-only `rest-server` keeps old backups from being deleted or overwritten by a server that's taken over, and is a natural future Roostack Cloud target. It doesn't stop that server *adding* files restic can't read, which can stop the repository working until they're removed (§10.10, "Junk in a repository").
- **Considered: Kopia.** It's faster, uses less memory (≈4 MB average chunks vs ≈1 MB, plus a memory-mapped index) and has a richer server. It's a realistic fallback if restic's memory or performance becomes a problem with very large libraries; the interface keeps switching cheap.
- **Expected resources** (estimate, ~750 GB / 100k photos and videos): ~150–400 MB RAM for nightly backups, up to ~1 GB for prune/check. Mitigations: `GOGC=20`, infrequent pruning, a systemd memory limit on backup jobs, quiet-hours scheduling.

**Backup process:**
1. **Prepare:** per-package hooks create a consistent state (e.g. `pg_dump` to `<service>/db-dump/`, SQLite's backup command, or a brief stop if there's no safe live method).
2. **Snapshot:** `/var/lib/roostack` plus each service's folder. Since 2026-10-04, one snapshot per service (tagged `service=<name>`, with its secrets) and one for the server's own state (tagged `server`): see §10.10, "Restoring across the lab".
3. **Verify:** periodic `restic check` and occasional test restores.
4. **Restore — Locked (2026-09-27):** per service, by the Owner, from the Backups page: choose one of the backups that contain the service (newest first), confirm, and it runs as an uninterruptible coordinator job with a live log. The service stops, its current data is moved aside (`<dir>.before-restore-<time>`), the backup's files and database dump come back, and it starts again. If getting the files back fails, the current data is put back (a partial restore is kept as `<dir>.failed-restore-<time>`) and the service starts again; if the data can't be put back, the service stays stopped and the log says where the data is, rather than claiming it's back (2026-09-29: a failed put-back used to go unnoticed and the service stayed stopped either way). Kept copies are listed on the restore page and deleted only when the Owner chooses. Restoring a whole lab onto a new server comes with the installer.

**Restore:** per service, or the full lab onto a new server (install Roostack → point at the backup → enter the recovery key).

**A restore that didn't finish — as built (2026-10-22), the Owner's choices (quality review P3):** a restore puts the service's files back, then loads its databases from the dumps among them. If that fails, starting it would run it on files without the databases that go with them (an app may lose track of what it keeps, or offer its first-run setup to whoever opens it). Since the security review of 2026-10-16 it was left stopped, but only until the next apply on its server or a reboot, which started every desired unit. Now the service is **held stopped in its desired state**: `held:` in its instance file (`instance.Hold`: the backup, when it was made, the error, and the data from before if there is any), which leaves its app's containers, address and ports out (`generate.Stop`, as for a withdrawn version; its databases keep running so they can be loaded again), on every apply and after a reboot.
  - **Where it's held.** A restore the controller does (from a copy, or onto another server) holds it as soon as the dumps fail. A server restoring its own service from its own main backup does it as a piece of its maintenance (`backup.Restores`), so the controller asks that server until the restore has ended and how (`GET /v1/restores/<service>/outcome`: `backup.RestoreOutcome`, `DumpsFailed` from a `backup.DumpsError`), then holds it. The server leaves it stopped meanwhile; if the controller restarts while a restore is running, nothing holds it afterwards (the old behaviour). Restores and moves stop and start a service by what runs of it now (`state.Output`), so a held service can be restored again.
  - **What people see.** The restore log ends "Immich's files are back from the backup of Mon 20 Oct 03:00, but its database couldn't be loaded, so Roostack is keeping it stopped. Choose what to do on Immich's page." Needs attention has a problem (emailed with problems): "Immich is stopped: its files are back from the backup of …, but its database couldn't be loaded.", linking "What to do"; its status is **Stopped**, with the hint "Its restore didn't finish". Its page says why it's stopped and what would happen if it started, with the error's first line and a link to the restore's log. Owners and Admins see it; only the Owner gets the choices ("The Owner can restore it again or start it anyway"):
    - **Restore again…** opens the restore page with the same backup chosen (`?backup=`), to try again once the cause is fixed, or an older one;
    - **Put back what was there…** (only with a kept copy) puts back the data from before the restore and starts it as it was; the backup's files are kept in turn;
    - **Start Immich anyway…** (a disclosure, then a danger button and a confirmation) starts it on the backup's files with whatever is in its databases.
  - Each choice, and a restore that works, ends the hold, recorded in the audit log ("Started Immich after its restore didn't finish"; "Restored Immich, so it's no longer stopped").
  - *Considered:* the server holding it itself, in a file of its own (no gap while the controller isn't listening, but a second place desired state lives); holding it from the moment the restore is asked for (no gap, but the service would stop while the restore waits behind a backup).

**Full-lab restore — as built (2026-09-28):** on a fresh install, the first-run page offers "Restore a lab from its backups" next to creating the Owner (both need the setup code). The Owner says where the backups are (an S3-compatible bucket, a disk mounted on the server, or any restic address) and enters the recovery key; Roostack looks for every server's repository there (`roostack/<lab>/<server>`, a few levels deep for a disk), opens each with the key and shows the lab, server and latest backup of those it opens. Only the controller's (or a single server's) backup can be restored: its files go back where they were (Roostack's state, Podman secrets, every service's data, owners included), the recovery key becomes the backup key, and Roostack restarts. At that start the backed-up copy of Roostack's database becomes the live one (same accounts, settings and server identity); once the first apply has set the services up again, each service with a database is stopped, loaded from its dump and started, while "needs attention" says so. Backups carry the servers' WireGuard keys (`wireguard-keys.json`, kept by Roostack like the CA key), and the agent reuses a saved key for an interface that has none, so a restored controller keeps the keys its nodes and friends' labs know; they reconnect if they can reach it the way they reached the old one. Backups then continue at the lab's configured destination. The recovery key typed on the restore pages is kept in `<state>/restore-key` while a restore is prepared; it's deleted when the restore finishes or the first Owner is created (an abandoned restore), and never backed up (2026-10-16). If a service's databases can't be loaded after its files are back (restoring one service), it's held stopped and says so, rather than starting half-restored (below, "A restore that didn't finish"). **A restore that fails part-way can be started again (2026-10-16):** a server counted as "not fresh" once it had instance files, which the server's own snapshot puts back first, so after a later failure the page said the server already ran services (review M25). Now `<state>/restore-started` marks a restore under way (removed when it finishes, or when the first Owner is created instead; never backed up); while it's there the server counts as fresh, and the restore page says the last restore didn't finish and offers starting again, which puts everything back again over what it restored. **What a restore trusts (2026-10-17, review M26):** a backup says which server made it and whether that's the controller (from the files in it), and every server holds a key to its own folder (a node its own key, the controller the recovery key; corrected 2026-10-19, it said every server holds the recovery key) and may write there, so a node could make a backup that says it's the controller: it was offered beside the real one, and restored any paths it listed. Now each backup found says whose folder it's in (each server's is named after its ID: `roostack/<lab>/<server>` on a disk or online storage, `labs/<lab>/<server>` in Roostack Cloud, `<server>/<lab>` on a lab's own backup store, where the first is the server allowed to write it); a controller's backup is offered only from that server's own folder, and otherwise shown as not offered, saying why (backups moved by hand go back in a folder named after the server); and a restore puts back only Roostack's state folder, the Podman secrets store and each service's own folders (`<services_dir>/<name>` and `<media_dir>/<name>`, as this server's lab.yaml sets them), refusing a backup that lists anything else before putting anything back. Still possible: a node's backup claiming to be the controller of a lab of its own (its own ID, in its own folder), shown with its server's name and folder for the person to tell apart. Considered: offering a controller only when there's one (a forged one would then hide the real one). Considered: restoring into a staging folder and renaming at the end (cleaner, but Roostack's state, the secrets store and each service's folders are on different paths, so there's no single rename). Not yet: restoring from storage at a friend's lab (its link is in the lost state), and restoring a node on its own (restore the controller, then move the node's services).

**Defaults & policy:**
- Nightly; keep 7 daily, 4 weekly and 6 monthly.
  - **Two copies of one lab — as built (2026-10-04):** a full-lab restore makes the new machine the same lab (IDs, keys, domain), so if the old one comes back (it was only offline), both would back up into the same repositories and update the same DNS records. Fencing: the lab has a generation (`lab-generation` in the state folder, backed up with it), which a full-lab restore raises; every snapshot is tagged `gen=<n>`; before uploading, a server lists its repository (if it can't, the backup fails and is tried again, rather than going ahead unchecked: since 2026-10-16), and a snapshot of a newer generation means this copy is out of date: it stands down (no backups anywhere, no DNS updates; a problem on Home and its Backups page, which explain). Its services aren't stopped: a false alarm mustn't take anything down. "This is the right copy → Keep this copy" (Owner) raises its generation past the other's, so the other stands down at its next backup (a test restore that fenced the real lab). The same mark serves a server the controller removed while it couldn't be reached (a gone server, once nothing waits on it): the controller adds a `fence=removed` snapshot to its repositories, and if it comes back it stands down and its own page says it was removed. Considered: a new ID for restored services (the clash is the lab's and its servers' identity, not a service's; and it would cut their backup history); stopping the old copy's services (a false alarm would take the lab down). Not covered: an old copy that can't reach its backups at all (it then can't harm them either; DNS is the remaining risk).
  - **When "nightly" isn't simple — as built (2026-10-04):** the time is the server's local 02:00. On the night clocks go forward it runs at 03:00 (02:00 doesn't exist), and on the night they go back at the second 02:00: once either way (checked with Australia/Sydney). The schedule looks at the clock every minute rather than waiting once, as Go's timers don't count time asleep: after a suspend (or a paused VM) the backup runs as the machine wakes, not hours late. A night the server was off is caught up: 10 minutes after Roostack starts, then hourly, a backup runs if none was tried for 26 hours (an attempt counts: failures have their own retries). The same check makes a new lab's first backup, within the hour of backups being set up rather than that night (2026-10-18): the next backup's time on Home and the Backups page says when the check will run it, and its log says "no backup yet" rather than "none for over a day". The controller's weekly clean-up tries a repository that's locked (its server still backing up, e.g. a big first backup) again an hour later, up to three times. Considered and left: starting each server's backup a few minutes apart (they share a destination only sometimes), and waiting for the clock to sync at boot (a machine without a clock battery; not a NUC).
- Packages classify data as **critical** (backed up by default: photos, passwords, repos) or **replaceable** (not backed up by default: media libraries, caches).
- **Destinations:** local disk, remote (S3/B2; SFTP planned, not built), and Roostack Cloud (below). **Local-only is allowed, with a persistent warning** recommending off-site.
  - **Roostack Cloud backup — as built (2026-10-13):** for a lab linked to Roostack Cloud, the chooser offers "Roostack Cloud: off-site, set up for you", with the lab's use of its space ("This lab uses 1.2 GB of 50 GB"). It is online storage in Australia that Cloud runs; each server gets its own folder there, and backups are encrypted with the recovery key before they leave, so Cloud can store them but not read them. Nothing to configure: no account, keys or address to type. How it works:
    - **Its space, on the backup and Home — as built (2026-10-19):** the lab asks `GET /v1/lab/storage` after each check-in (daily; Cloud measures daily) and keeps the answer in `link.json` (`Status.Storage`), so pages never wait on Cloud (the backup card reloads every 10 seconds). The backup's card says "your lab uses 8.5 GB of the 10.0 GB in your plan (85%)", and from 80%, the level at which Cloud emails the account, and again at 100%, the card and Home's attention list say so ("Roostack Cloud backup is 85% full: this lab's backups there take…"; at 100% "is full", and that Cloud will be in touch about more space, as its email says), as warnings, since backups carry on. The quota is the account's, which its labs share. **The account's whole use — as built (2026-10-19):** `cloudapi.StorageUsage.AccountUsedBytes` (optional) is what the account's linked labs store together, as Cloud's quota emails count it; the card, the chooser and the warnings go by it, and name this lab's share when others take some too ("your account's labs use 8.5 GB of the 10.0 GB in your plan (85%), this lab 3.0 GB"; "Roostack Cloud backup is 85% full: your account's labs take 8.5 GB of the 10.0 GB in your plan, this lab's backups 3.0 GB."). A Cloud from before it sends none, and the lab shows its own use as before; a Roostack from before it ignores the field. Until then, an account with several labs could be past 80% before one lab's figure was. Considered: asking Cloud as each page loads (slow pages, and nothing when Cloud is down); the other labs' names and shares (Cloud's emails have them; a lab needn't know its account's other labs).
    - **Short-lived keys.** No server keeps a storage password. Before each backup a server asks for keys that last 12 hours and open only its own folder, and only to add backups (they can't delete). The controller asks Cloud over its link (§4); any other server asks the controller over the lab API (`POST /v1/storage-keys`, mutual TLS), so nodes never talk to Cloud themselves. Keys are kept 0600 in the state folder (`cloud-backup-keys.json`), renewed when under an hour is left, and used while more than 5 minutes are left if Cloud can't be reached meanwhile. They're left out of the lab's own backups (they expire, so a copy is no use).
    - **The lab prunes, Cloud never holds a restic key.** Like every destination, the controller prunes each server's repository weekly with the recovery key. For that it asks Cloud for the lab's prune keys (`cloud-prune-keys.json`), which may delete within the lab's folders only. Cloud keeps deleted and overwritten files for 90 days, so neither a compromised server nor a stolen prune key can destroy backups at once.
    - Considered: one long-lived storage key per server (a key leaked from a backup or a stolen disk would work until someone noticed), and Cloud pruning for the lab (Cloud would need a key that opens the backups, which breaks "Cloud can't read it"). restic exits 0 when a delete is refused, so tests check the snapshots, not the exit code.
    - Not yet: a second, archived copy kept by Cloud (see TASKS).
  - **Restoring from Roostack Cloud — as built (2026-10-13), the user agreed the design the same day:** after losing the controller (a fire, a dead disk), a fresh install's "Restore a lab" page offers "Roostack Cloud", which needs only the recovery key: no account login, address or keys. It rests on the lab's **recovery identity**, an Ed25519 key derived from the recovery key (HKDF-SHA256; the key is 256 random bits, so no slow password hashing is needed). The controller registers the public half with Cloud when it links, at each daily check-in if it changed, and as soon as a new recovery key replaces the old (the old key then stops working with Cloud too). **Since 2026-10-17 (review M10) the registration proves the key:** it carries a signature over the lab's ID with its own purpose ("register", beside "access" and "relink" below), and Cloud refuses one without it, so a lab that learned another's public key can't register it and have that lab's restores find its own backups instead. Signing the lab's ID rather than a challenge from Cloud saves a round trip and ties the signature to the one lab already; nothing was released, so there's no period that still accepts unsigned ones. To restore, Roostack asks Cloud for a challenge, signs it, and gets keys that read the lab's folders (12 hours, as a big restore takes hours; they may write only restic's locks) and the list of its servers' repositories; the usual search and restore follow. **Every lab with the key (2026-10-17, review M10):** a lab unlinked from Cloud and linked again is a new lab there with the same recovery key, and Cloud keeps the old one's backups for a while. Cloud gave only one lab (the linked one), so the older backups couldn't be reached; now it gives each lab with the key, the linked one first, then the latest unlinked, each with its own keys and repositories. The restore page shows each lab, with its name and when it was linked or unlinked, and its backups under it, and the person chooses. A lab restored from an unlinked lab's backups links again from its Settings (Cloud refuses its old link). Considered: Cloud choosing the newest lab's backups (the person restoring knows which moment they want; the newest may be a test install). The recovery key never leaves the server. Anyone with the recovery key can already decrypt every backup, so reading them gives nothing more away; Cloud still emails the account each time, with where from and what to do if it wasn't them (change the recovery key).
    - **Relinking:** the restored controller's backup holds its Cloud certificate, which usually still works (certificates last 90 days and renewing doesn't cancel the old one). When Cloud refuses it (expired while the lab was off, or a restore from an old backup), the daily check signs a challenge to get a new certificate instead of forgetting the link, at once rather than after a waiting period (the user's choice: someone restoring after a fire needs their address back now; the email lets the real owner react). It must be the same lab, and Cloud refuses it for a lab that was unlinked on purpose: that one links again as usual. Its backups can still be read while Cloud keeps them.
    - Signatures are for one purpose ("access", "relink" or "register" is part of what's signed), each challenge works once for 5 minutes, and proving is rate-limited in Cloud, per address and per lab (2026-10-13). A relink refused because Cloud is busy (rate limited, or failing) keeps the link and tries again at the next check; only a refusal forgets it.
    - Considered: logging into the Cloud account to restore (it needs no new cryptography, but the person restoring often isn't the account's owner, and account recovery may be the very thing lost; it may come later as a second way); Cloud holding a copy of the recovery key (it could then read the backups, which breaks "Cloud can't read it").
- **3-2-1 backups — Locked (requirement), Open (design), 2026-09-26:** Roostack should guide users towards 3-2-1 (3 copies of the data, on 2 different kinds of storage, 1 off-site), not just warn. That means:
  - **Suggestions in the UI** explaining what's missing and why, e.g. "Your backups are only on this server" → suggest a USB disk, then an off-site destination.
  - **Multiple backup destinations**, e.g. a local disk *and* an off-site repository. **As built (2026-09-28):** each server has a main destination (backup 1) and any number of **extra copies** elsewhere (backups 2, 3, …, in the plan below). The controller creates each copy's repository like the main one (recovery key plus a key of the server's own). Each copy is **its own backup** of the same files, made right after the main one (the database dumps are already fresh), rather than `restic copy`: restic reads both repositories' credentials from the same environment variables, so two S3 accounts or two backup stores couldn't be told apart, and separate backups keep each copy readable on its own. restic's file cache keeps the second run cheap. Copies still run when the main destination fails. Opening each repository is limited to 2 minutes (2026-10-03): restic keeps retrying storage it can't reach, such as another server that's switched off, which held up the whole run. **Destinations run one after another — Locked (2026-10-03):** one set of database dumps for all of them, and at the same time they'd compete for the same disk reads and (at home) upload bandwidth, so it wouldn't be quicker; one failing doesn't stop the rest, and a main destination whose drive isn't plugged in is skipped while the copies go ahead. **Trying again — Locked (2026-10-03):** only the destinations that failed are tried again, after 1 hour and then 3 hours, then they wait for the nightly backup (each retry makes fresh database dumps); failures that need someone (a wrong key, a full destination, no permission) aren't retried, and a destination whose drive isn't plugged in is backed up as soon as it is (checked every minute). The card says when ("Roostack tries again today 14:00."), and "needs attention" waits until the retries are over (at once for a missing drive, which needs someone). The plan is kept in memory: after a restart the nightly backup tries everything anyway. Considered: retrying the whole run (repeats what worked), and retrying everything indefinitely (hides failures that need someone). Each copy's result is recorded and shown; a failed or stale copy needs attention. **Results belong to the destination — as built (2026-10-19):** each run is recorded with the repository it went to (`backup_runs.repository`), and every page (the plan, Home, the backup cards, "Needs attention", the weekly email, a service's last backup) reads a backup's results by its repository. Before, runs were recorded by role ("" for the main backup, else a copy's name), so right after "Make this backup 1" the new main backup showed the old one's last result until the next run. The plan matches its entries to results by repository too, so a page read before the server has restarted with a swap is right as well. Older runs were given the repository their role had in `lab.yaml` at the first start since (`store.AdoptBackupRuns`, once), which is what the pages showed before; runs of a copy since stopped keep none, and weren't shown before either. A copy's runs now record the services they backed up too, as the main backup's did, so a copy that becomes the main backup says which services it has. Considered: renaming the old rows at each swap (keeps the role as the key, so changing where the main backup goes would still show the old place's results). Copies are pruned and checked with the main repository. Stopping a copy leaves the copies made so far.
  - **Your backup plan — Locked (2026-09-28, numbering revised the same day):** the Backups page and the wizard's backup step show the same plan, numbered as the 3-2-1 rule counts copies. It opens with a one-line summary of the rule ("Keep 3 copies of your data, on 2 kinds of storage, with 1 away from home…") and a "Tell me more" that explains each number and why. **1 is the data on the server itself**, always there (not a backup, but the first of the three copies). **2 is the main backup** (restores read it first; best at home, where restoring is quick: the plan suggests "Make this the main backup" for an at-home backup when the main one isn't at home). **3 is a second backup.** Any more are **extra backups**, optional and not numbered (a stacked-copies icon instead). Empty places for 2 and 3 are dashed, with a suggestion that adapts to what's there (none yet: "a drive at home, like a USB drive or a NAS"; nothing away from home: "online storage, or space at a friend's lab"; only disks: "not a disk like the one your data is on"); an optional extra-backup place is always offered after them. Backups are added in order: only the next place has "Add". Each card shows its kind (USB drive, second disk, network share, this server's disk, folder, online storage, a friend's lab, another Roostack instance), at home or away, and its last result, and the **space** it takes where known: the size of its repository (restic `stats --mode raw-data`, as stored: deduplicated and compressed), recorded after each successful backup so pages never wait on it; the free space on the drive, for this server's drives; and for a friend's lab, the whole lab's use of its space there. Another server's free space and online storage's aren't shown. A backup on the server's own disk carries a warning: it helps with mistakes, but a failed disk or ransomware on the server loses the data and that backup together. Under "Manage": where it is (the repository), change where the main backup goes (a new backup starts there, the old stay), make another backup the main one (the two swap; both keep their backups and keys), or stop a backup. One **chooser** adds a backup anywhere: a drive (the drive picker below, or a typed folder for technical users), online storage (a saved account or a new one), a friend's lab (their storage, or a new invite), or another Roostack instance's disk (not in the wizard); for another server, "its own disk" (drives plugged into it are listed on its own page for now). Where a server's backups already go isn't offered again; this server's own disk counts once. A backup that fails to set up is forgotten again (the one before it is put back), and the failure stays shown on the plan for an hour. **Layout — as built (2026-10-18, the Owner's request):** the places are one under another at every width, and adding happens in the box it's for: the chooser opens there, and once it's sent that box shows the setting-up activity until it ends (a failure then shows above the list, as before). Tiles side by side (2026-10-15) read wrong (the numbers ran across, with room to spare) and moved a box being added to a row of its own, away from its place. Adding a copy (or changing the main backup) restarts Roostack, whose activity logs live in memory: if the page's status line asks after the restart, it's told to reload (`HX-Refresh`) rather than getting "not found", which htmx ignored, leaving the last step spinning for good (found 2026-10-18 adding Roostack Cloud as a copy). **Names on a lab of one server (2026-10-18):** setting up, copying, pruning, restoring and key changes say "this server", "the backups" or nothing where they named it ("Asking Roostack Cloud for a folder for this server"): its name is the host's, which the Owner never chose. With several servers they name it ("…for nas's folder (one of My lab's servers)"). Under the plan, the **checks**: 3 copies (the data and at least two backups), 2 kinds of storage (the data is on a disk, so at least one backup that isn't a disk: disks count as one kind, whether this server's, plugged into it, a folder, or another server's), 1 away from home (online storage, a friend's lab, or another server that's somewhere else), and backed up lately. **Where each server is — Locked (2026-09-28):** Roostack can't tell whether a server is at home or somewhere else (a VPS, a relative's house), so the Owner says, once per server: asked on the plan's card when a backup goes to another server ("Where is vps? At home / Somewhere else"), and changeable on Settings → Servers. It's kept on the controller. A backup on another server is at home when both servers are at home, and away otherwise (different places, or both away from home); until both are known it counts as neither. Detecting it from the lab network was considered and not chosen: it can guess wrong (VPNs, a VPS reached over a home tunnel). The wizard's step needs the main backup to continue (or "Set up backups later"); the others are optional there and can be added later.
  - **1 safe from ransomware — Locked (2026-10-16):** a fifth check beside the 3-2-1 ones: at least one backup that this server can't delete, so ransomware on it, or someone who takes it over, can't destroy every backup. Which destinations count is in §10.10 ("What a server can delete"). Every backup the server *can* delete says so on its card ("If this server were taken over, its backups here could be deleted too"), with a suggestion: for online storage holding the account's key, bucket versioning with a rule keeping old versions for 30 days and a key that can't delete old versions, or Backblaze B2; for drives and folders, another backup it can't delete. The server's own disk keeps its own warning instead. A Backblaze backup says its key can add but not erase, and warns when the bucket erases hidden files in under 30 days. "Tell me more" explains the check; with the other checks met, the next empty place suggests a backup safe from ransomware. Considered: folding it into "1 away from home". Not chosen: a USB drive at home can be safe from fire but not from ransomware, and the reverse holds for online storage with the account's key. The label was chosen with the Owner (2026-10-16). Review finding H1 (2026-10-16). *Since 2026-10-17* online storage other than Backblaze counts too when Roostack's check shows the bucket keeps old versions that the account's key can't erase, for 30 days or more (§10.10, "Checking an S3 bucket"); its card then says so, and a bucket that keeps versions the key *can* erase says to give Roostack a key that can't.
  - **Backup drives — Locked (2026-09-28):** nobody types a path. `roostack-agent` lists the server's drives (from `lsblk` and the mounted network shares) and the plan's chooser (wizard and Backups page) shows them as cards, best first: a **USB drive** or **second disk**, then a **network share**, then **this server's own disk** (marked: it protects against mistakes, not a failed disk). The list refreshes by itself every few seconds, so a drive plugged in appears. Choosing a drive mounts it for good by its filesystem's UUID in `/etc/fstab` (`nofail`, `x-systemd.automount`: a missing drive never holds up booting, and one unplugged and plugged back in is mounted again when the backup next uses it), under `/mnt/roostack-<name>`, with a `roostack` folder the roostack user owns (exFAT, FAT and NTFS drives are mounted so Roostack owns their files). A disk with nothing usable on it (new, or an unknown filesystem) can be **erased and prepared** (one GPT partition, ext4, labelled ROOSTACK), after a confirmation naming the disk, model and size, as long as it isn't mounted or listed in `/etc/fstab` (the agent checks that itself since 2026-10-16, so an idle backup drive is never erased); the system's disk, and LVM, RAID and encrypted disks, are never offered. The repository records the drive's UUID, and every backup first checks **that drive is connected and mounted** there, otherwise it fails with "the backup drive … isn't connected" instead of filling the server's own disk. Technical users can instead type a folder under `/mnt`, `/media` or `/srv` (it must already exist, and the agent makes only a `roostack` folder inside it: changed 2026-10-16, review of that day, so the roostack user can no longer have folders made at any depth). The roostack user can write in `/srv/roostack` and so could plant a symlink there, so the agent makes and hands over these folders only through an `os.Root` on the allowed place and with `lchown`: nothing is created or changes owner outside it, even if a symlink appears midway (2026-09-29: it used to check the path only after creating the folders, which could have given roostack a folder anywhere, such as a systemd drop-in folder for the agent). The full-lab restore page lists connected drives the same way.
  - **Phase 2 — adding NAS shares from the UI (agreed 2026-09-28, not built yet, see TASKS):** shares already mounted on the server are offered today. Next: add an SMB or NFS share from the wizard (server, share, user and password kept in a root-only credentials file, mounted by the agent like a drive, `nofail` and automounted), with a check that Roostack can write there.
  - **Later — rotating drives (agreed 2026-09-28, design leaves room):** two or more drives swapped in turn (one kept away from home). Each drive would be a destination of its own, recognised by its UUID, with "the other drive is at home, last backed up …" shown; not built yet.
  - The dashboard shows the status of each destination, so a quietly failing off-site copy is noticed.
- **Recovery key:** shown at setup; the user must save or print it, with a strong prompt for the person doing the setup to keep a copy. Optional key escrow via Roostack Cloud later.
  - **What it looks like — Locked (2026-10-19, the user's choice), as built the same day:** 32 random bytes as 64 lowercase hex digits in 16 groups of four joined by dashes (`3f9a-07c2-…`, 79 characters), made by `backup.NewRecoveryKey` for the first key, a changed key, and `roostack backup init`. People write it down and type it back: hex has no letters to confuse (`O`/`0`, `l`/`1`) and no case, and the groups keep the place. A typed key is tidied (`backup.TypedRecoveryKey`): any 64 hex digits, with or without dashes or spaces, in either case, become the grouped form, which is the restic password and what the recovery identity is derived from; anything else is only trimmed, so keys made before still work. Until then the first key was 32 bytes in base64url (43 characters, mixed case with `-` and `_`) and a changed one 24 bytes in plain hex: two helpers, by accident (found 2026-10-19). Considered: plain hex (64 characters with nothing to keep your place), base64 (shorter, but awkward to copy by hand), a word list (easiest to read aloud, but longer and needs a list kept with the code).
  - **Shown when it's made — as built (2026-09-29):** the lab's first backup makes the key, and the Owner is shown it and must confirm saving it, whether the backup was added in the wizard or later on the Backups page (until then, choosing "Set up backups later" and adding one afterwards made a key nobody was shown). The setup page (`/setup/recovery-key`) works only until it's confirmed; confirming restarts Roostack only if a wizard step's settings are waiting for it.
  - **Showing it again — Locked (2026-09-29):** the Owner can see it again from the Backups page ("Show recovery key…"), after re-entering their password and an unused two-factor code, even though logged in: the key opens every backup and keeps working after a password change, so an open or stolen session shouldn't be enough. Each showing and each failed attempt is audited; five wrong attempts end the session (so a stolen session can't be used to guess the password); the page isn't cached. Considered: not showing it again at all (people lose the paper, and a key nobody has makes the backups useless); showing it with no re-check. **Changing it — Locked (2026-09-30), see "Changing the recovery key" below.**
  - **Changing the recovery key — Locked (2026-09-30, simplified the same day):** "Change recovery key…" on the Backups page, after the same password and code check as showing it: a new key is made and shown, the Owner confirms saving it, then Roostack adds it to every repository (main, extras, other servers' repositories where it is the second key, storage at a friend's lab through the controller's admin connection), checks it opens each, and only then removes the old key. Fast, keeps the history, and one key opens everything. A repository that can't be reached (a drive unplugged, a friend's lab offline) stays "waiting" with the old key still valid there, shown on the Backups page, and is finished when it's back. There is never a moment without a key the Owner has confirmed saving. Audited.
    - A restic key only wraps the repository's master key, so a new key doesn't protect copies someone already made of the backups; the page and help say so.
    - Considered and dropped (2026-09-30): asking "could someone also have your backups?" and then starting every backup afresh with a new master key. It made changing the key complicated, and costs a full re-upload and double the space for months, for a rare case. Also considered: new backups use the new key and old ones keep the old key (the Owner would need both keys until the old backups age out). **Later, if needed:** a separate "Start my backups afresh" (new repositories for new backups, with the new key also added to the old ones so one key still opens everything), not part of changing the key.
    - **As built (2026-09-30):** "Change recovery key…" on the Backups page; after the password and code, the new key is shown (and again only after another check) until the Owner ticks "I've saved it", or cancels. Then a maintenance job (one at a time with backups) adds it to every repository the controller reaches for pruning, plus its own main backup, checking the new key opens each; when all have it, the key files swap (the old one kept as `backup-key.old`), and the old key is removed from each (by the ID noted when adding). Unreachable repositories are retried hourly. Progress is in `backup-key.change.json`; all `backup-key.*` files are left out of the lab's backups. The page says plainly that this doesn't protect copies someone already has.
- **Large first uploads:** resumable, with progress and an estimated finish time shown in the UI, a configurable bandwidth limit, and possibly a "seed locally, then take off-site" option.

### 6.10 Host Security — Locked

Layered defaults:

1. **Firewall:** default-deny inbound. Allow 80/443 plus ports that specific services need (e.g. WireGuard, Pi-hole DNS on the LAN). `roostack-agent` generates a readable **nftables** ruleset file and reloads it (nftables is Debian 13's default, so no extra dependency).
   - **SSH access — Locked (2026-09-27):** the Owner chooses in the setup wizard (step 2) and in Settings: home network only (recommended, default), anywhere, or nobody. The page lists current SSH connections (read from `/proc/net/tcp`) and a choice that would cut one off must be confirmed. Stored in `lab.yaml` (`firewall.ssh`).
   - Roostack applies the firewall after every apply, so enabled package ports are opened (home network only unless public). Until the SSH question is answered, Roostack leaves the firewall alone.
   - **Who counts as home — as built (2026-10-16, after the security review's H3, H4 and M23; the lab's servers taken out 2026-10-17):** "home network" (SSH "home only", private package ports, Caddy's private routes and the UI) is the private ranges (RFC 1918, ULA, link-local, loopback) and the CGNAT range 100.64.0.0/10 (Tailscale, people's devices on Roostack's network later), **except other labs' links (100.100.0.0/14), Roostack Cloud's relay (100.81.0.0/16) and the lab's own servers (100.96.0.0/17, §10.2)**, which are never home. A Tailscale device whose address falls in one of those ranges isn't home either (Tailscale picks addresses across 100.64.0.0/10, so roughly one device in twelve: mostly the other labs' /14, since 2026-10-16); Tailscale's admin console can change a device's address. The firewall's set and Caddy's list are both made from `trust.HomeRanges` (below). Interfaces are settled before any rule by source: from `roostack-relay` only Caddy's edge listener; from `roostack` (the lab's servers) only what's opened for servers (§10.2); from `roostack-guests` only the guest API and each guest lab's own store, from its own address; `roostack-st*` (this lab's links to other labs' storage) take nothing in; nothing is forwarded to or from any of them. Before this, a friend's lab reached SSH, home-only ports, the UI and private routes, since its addresses were inside the CGNAT block. Kept: the CGNAT range as home for Tailscale users (the Owner's choice, 2026-10-16); considered dropping it (safer where an ISP lets CGNAT subscribers reach each other, but it would cut off Tailscale).
   - **One place for trust classes — as built (2026-10-17, review §6):** H3, H4 and M23 had one root cause: who counts as what was a set of address ranges written out in four places (the agent's ruleset, `internal/firewall.Desired`, `node.Local`'s inline rules and Caddy's list). Now `internal/trust` defines the classes (anyone, home, the lab's servers, a server still joining, an edge, other labs, the relay, this lab's links to other labs' storage), how each is recognised (an interface first, then an address: WireGuard guarantees which peer an address on its interface is) and what each may reach. Every firewall rule names the class it's for (`Rule.For`: anyone, home, servers or guests; the agent renders each by its interface and addresses), `firewall.Desired` builds them all (the server's roles, such as a backup store or storage for other labs, come from `node.Local` as `firewall.Roles`), and Caddy's private list is made from the same home ranges. Tests keep them equal: one runs a source of every class through a small evaluator of the generated nftables rules and compares each port's verdict with `trust.May`; another checks every edge of every range against Caddy's list. *Considered:* the agent owning it (it knows its interfaces, as the review suggested), but Caddy's generator would then import the agent's server code; a small package beside it is read by both.
2. **Admin UI is LAN/VPN-only by default.** Public access requires explicit opt-in **and** mandatory 2FA. Rationale: the UI controls the whole host and is the highest-value target.
3. **SSH hardening — Locked, as built (2026-10-17):** key-only authentication, no password login, no root login. The user can opt out, but **the UI shows a persistent warning** while SSH takes passwords. Found promised but missing in the security review of 2026-10-16 (M19); built the next day, with the Owner choosing each point below from a mock-up (2026-10-17).
   - **Where it's asked:** with who can log in (item 1), in setup step 2 and in **Settings → Remote access**, for each server: "How do they log in?", **With an SSH key only** (recommended) or **With a key or a password**. Stored in `lab.yaml` (`firewall.ssh_login`: `key` or `password`). Not asked on a server with no SSH server (openssh-server not installed). It's the wizard, not the installer, that asks (this item first said the installer): apt runs the installer without anyone to answer, and it can't check a key.
   - **What keys only is:** one drop-in, `/etc/ssh/sshd_config.d/10-roostack.conf`, with `PasswordAuthentication no`, `KbdInteractiveAuthentication no` and `PermitRootLogin no`, written or removed by `roostack-agent` (`PUT /v1/ssh`, which takes only on or off; the content is the agent's own). Debian's `sshd_config` includes that folder at its top and sshd keeps the first value it reads, so `10-` wins over cloud images' `50-cloud-init.conf`. The agent checks every change with `sshd -t`; if sshd refuses it (another file is broken), the old file is put back and SSH isn't reloaded, so it keeps running as it was; otherwise `systemctl try-reload-or-restart ssh.service`. Logged-in sessions stay. Roostack doesn't write it again at start: removed by hand, the page says it's missing and the warning shows.
   - **No root at all (the Owner's choice):** `PermitRootLogin no`, not `prohibit-password` (Debian's default, root by key): a stolen key alone then doesn't give root where sudo asks a password. A cloud server that only has root (Hetzner, DigitalOcean) needs an account made first; the help shows the commands. *Considered:* root by key only (no extra account on cloud servers, but less than promised).
   - **How Roostack knows a key works (the Owner's choice):** keys only needs sshd's log to show a key login (`Accepted publickey`) by an account that still exists, isn't root, and is in the `sudo` group: proof that someone holds a working key and can still get root. Only journal lines from root running sshd's own program count (`_UID=0`, `_EXE=/usr/lib/openssh/sshd-session` or `/usr/sbin/sshd`), which journald fills in itself: any user can log a line that says it's sshd's (`logger -t sshd-session`). Until one is seen, the page says so, links to "Set up an SSH key" in the help and offers **check again**. *Considered:* an `authorized_keys` entry for a sudo account (doesn't prove anyone holds the private key or that sshd accepts the file's permissions, and the agent would need to read `/home`), a test connection (Roostack has no private key to test with), and the Owner ticking "my key works" (how people lock themselves out).
   - **"Nobody" (the Owner's choice):** keys only straight away, without the check: nothing reaches SSH, so nobody can be locked out, and a firewall mistake or opening SSH later finds passwords off. No warning. *Considered:* leaving sshd alone until SSH is opened.
   - **The default (the Owner's choice):** keys only when a key login has been seen (or it's already on); otherwise **With a key or a password**, and the wizard carries on, with the warning lasting until a key is set up. *Considered:* not letting the wizard go on with SSH open until a key works (safest, but it stops people who never use SSH unless they choose "Nobody").
   - **The lasting warning:** on the home page's Needs attention, for each server whose SSH takes passwords and can be reached: "SSH on this server still accepts passwords: anyone on your home network can try to guess one." (a warning), or "… accepts passwords from anywhere, so anyone who can reach it can try to guess one." (a problem), with **Turn passwords off**. Settings sums it up ("SSH from your home network only, passwords allowed"). It's worked out from what sshd really does (`sshd -T`, read through the agent and kept 30 seconds for pages that poll; the remote access page reads it fresh), so a file of the Owner's turning passwords back on is caught too, and a cloud image that already turned them off shows none.
   - **`Match` blocks — as built (2026-10-17):** a `Match` block overrides the global settings for the connections it matches, Roostack's file included (a drop-in can't hold a `Match` of its own: Debian includes the folder at the top of `sshd_config`, so everything after it would become part of the block). So the agent reads the `Match` lines of `sshd_config` and the files it includes and, when there are any, asks `sshd -T -C user=…,host=…,addr=…` about connections from an address in each home range, the lab's servers' range and the internet, and from each address and user the blocks name (a range's first address, a pattern's wildcards as 1, a group's members), to port 22 or a local address or port a block names, up to 256. Passwords for a home address (or a server's) count as "takes passwords"; for addresses outside home only, they matter once SSH is open to anyone. The page names the block ("SSH still takes passwords from some addresses: “Match Address 192.168.7.0/24” in its settings turns them back on…") when keys only is on, and the lasting warning counts it. *Not covered:* `Match Host` (it needs reverse DNS) and `RDomain`, and wildcard user names other than the one Roostack makes up. Checked on VM4 (docs/ASSUMPTIONS.md J5).
   - **Socket-activated SSH — checked (2026-10-17):** Debian 13's `ssh.socket` (`Accept=no`) starts the normal `ssh.service` on the first connection, so sshd reads its files when it starts, not per connection. Two things broke and were fixed: an sshd started by the socket can't reload (on SIGHUP it starts itself again and can't bind port 22, which systemd holds; the service fails and the agent reported an error, though the next connection then started it with the new files), so with `ssh.socket` active the agent restarts `ssh.service` instead (logged-in sessions stay); and while it waits for its first connection, `/run/sshd` (the service's `RuntimeDirectory`) is gone, so `sshd -t` and `sshd -T` fail, so the agent checks with `sshd -G` then (it reads the same files and refuses a broken one, without host keys or that folder) and reads the global settings with it, saying it couldn't check `Match` blocks (`sshd -G` takes no `-C`). docs/ASSUMPTIONS.md J4.
4. **Intrusion protection: CrowdSec**, enabled automatically once any service is public. It uses a Caddy module ("bouncer") to block at the proxy, and scenarios for Caddy and SSH logs. Not run on fully private labs (little benefit, saves resources).
   - **fail2ban is not run alongside it.** CrowdSec covers the same local log-parsing and banning, and running both would mean duplicate bans, two configs, and two tools changing the firewall.
   - **Signal sharing: on by default, opt-out.** Opting out is a **prominent choice when something first goes public** (changed 2026-09-30 from "during install", since CrowdSec only runs from then on; §6.11), and can be changed later in Settings. Only attack data is shared (attacker IPs, attack types, timestamps), nothing about the user's own users or data.
   - **If the user opts out:** local detection and banning keep working fully, like fail2ban but with better parsers and blocking at the proxy. They lose the community blocklist, i.e. pre-emptive blocking of IPs already seen attacking others. Rule and parser updates from the CrowdSec Hub are separate and still work.
   - **Web application firewall (WAF) — Locked (2026-10-06), step 1 as built (2026-10-19):** adopted, not built from scratch. A WAF reads requests (paths, headers, bodies), so it runs where TLS ends: in the lab's own Caddy, in front of each public service. That protects every public lab, whether its traffic comes through port forwarding, its own VPS or an edge (an edge never decrypts, §10.5).
     - **First: CrowdSec's AppSec component, on by default for public services.** Its "virtual patching" rules block exploits of known vulnerabilities in popular apps (often before people update), with few false positives since they target specific exploits. It uses what's already running (CrowdSec and its Caddy bouncer, item 4).
       - **The bouncer — checked (2026-10-19):** the pinned `caddy-crowdsec-bouncer` v0.14.1 already has an `appsec` handler (its `appsec` package, now built into Roostack's Caddy image beside `http`, at the same version), so no version change. It's set lab-wide in the `crowdsec` block (`appsec_url`), and each public route's `route` block runs `appsec` after the bans and rate limits, so a flood doesn't reach it. It sends AppSec the request's address, method, path, headers and body, over the server's loopback; Roostack caps the body at its first 1 MiB (`appsec_max_body_bytes`): uncapped, the bouncer reads the whole body into memory first, and CrowdSec refuses (drops) any body over 10 MB, which would refuse every photo or video upload. The rest of a body still reaches the service unchecked.
       - **CrowdSec:** an AppSec acquisition (`appsec.yaml`, listening on the container's 7422, published on `127.0.0.1:8475`) with the Hub's `crowdsecurity/appsec-default` settings, which run the collections `crowdsecurity/appsec-virtual-patching` (about 200 rules, one per exploit, `vpatch-*`) and `crowdsecurity/appsec-generic-rules` in band, blocking what they match with a 403. The generic rules are safe by default, so they're on: three narrow ones (FreeMarker template injection, PHP files run or listed from WordPress's uploads), the collection's "experimental" rule (no User-Agent) runs out of band and only logs, and its test rule matches one made-up path. Not used: `appsec-crs` (the Core Rule Set, step 2) and the bot challenges. Two different rules from one address within a minute ban it for 4 hours (the Hub's `appsec-vpatch` scenario).
       - **Pinned (2026-10-19), like everything else (§8.1):** the Hub's items have versions but `cscli` can't install a given one; it installs what the Hub's index says. So the whole Hub is pinned to one commit of `github.com/crowdsecurity/hub` (`generate.CrowdSecHub`), set in `config.yaml.local` (CrowdSec reads it over its own `config.yaml`): `cscli` fetches the index from that commit on GitHub, and checks every item it installs against the sha256 the index gives. The index's file is named after the commit, so a new commit is fetched when the container next starts (a changed CrowdSec file now restarts it), and the start's `hub upgrade` moves every item to it; the Caddy collection, unpinned until now, is pinned with it. A new commit comes with a Roostack release, so new virtual patches reach labs with Roostack's updates, not the day CrowdSec publishes them. *Not pinned, and can't be (checked 2026-10-19):* the data files some items download from `hub-data.crowdsec.net` (12 lists the Caddy scenarios use, such as `web/backdoors.txt` and `web/bad_user_agents.regex.txt`, and 7 address lists of Cloudflare's and search engines' crawlers that start as the image's copies; none for the AppSec rules; seen on VM2, docs/ASSUMPTIONS.md P12). `cscli` downloads each item's data from the URL written in the item, with no hash to check it against, and every `hub upgrade` (each start of the container) fetches any the server says changed since (CrowdSec 1.8.1's `hubops/download.go`); the site serves the latest of `github.com/crowdsecurity/sec-lists`, not a commit. There's no setting for the address, and changing the URL in an item changes the item, which the pinned index's hash then refuses as "tainted" and never upgrades. They're lists of patterns and addresses for detection, not code: a bad list could miss attacks or ban visitors wrongly, but not run anything. *Considered:* Roostack copying the 12 items with URLs pinned to a `sec-lists` commit (the rules copied into Roostack, rejected below); Roostack writing the files itself with a modification time in the future so `cscli` keeps them (relies on how its downloader compares dates); skipping `hub upgrade` (the Hub's commit would then never move). *Considered:* the Hub's live index (new patches within days, but the one thing on a lab that would change without a Roostack release, from a source nobody checked); copying the rules into Roostack (pinned, but every patch a Roostack change, and CrowdSec's own updates of them lost).
       - **Who it checks:** every public route, and Roostack's own pages when they're public (§6.12), for **visitors from outside only**, by the same check as private routes (a visitor through an edge or the relay is always outside). Home devices aren't checked: whoever can reach the private routes is trusted with them already, CrowdSec never bans a home address either, and a rule that wrongly matches the household's requests doesn't lock it out. Private services aren't affected.
       - **Off for a service, only by the Owner:** a package can't turn it off (pkgspec has no say; per-package exclusions are step 2's). An Owner can, per service (all its public addresses), on the address's public access page: under **Blocking known exploits**, "It refused something … needs", a red confirm whose question is the warning ("Requests that try one will reach it: if it has a vulnerability you haven't updated yet, anyone on the internet can use it."). It's audited, kept as `no_waf: true` in the instance file, and while it's off the page and the service's card say so, with **Block known exploits** to turn it back on. The UI, when public, can't be turned off: an SSH tunnel reaches it as a private route, unchecked. *Considered:* per address (most services have one; one switch is simpler to explain); a lab-wide switch (one service's false positive would unprotect them all).
       - **When AppSec is down: fail open (2026-10-19).** If AppSec doesn't answer within the bouncer's 2 seconds, or answers with an error, the request goes through (`appsec_fail_open`), like the bans when CrowdSec's API is down (ASSUMPTIONS P5). Reasons: AppSec runs inside the CrowdSec container, which restarts with every change to it (a Hub commit, the sharing choice) and on its first start downloads the Hub, so failing closed would take every public service down each time, for minutes; for most labs (a photo library at someone's parents') availability is what people notice, and virtual patching is a layer over updates, not the only defence; and to bypass it an attacker would have to make AppSec fail, which the rate limit in front of it makes hard. It isn't silent: Roostack's protection check is CrowdSec's API *and* AppSec listening (a connection it keeps open: pasta accepts one on the port even with nothing behind it, ASSUMPTIONS P10), so Settings → Public access and the checklist say "CrowdSec isn't running", and a public address's DNS record waits for both. *Considered:* fail closed (the bouncer's default; safer against an attacker who can crash AppSec, but every CrowdSec restart becomes an outage); a choice for the Owner (one more question few could answer).
       - **What it refused — as built (2026-10-19):** each request AppSec refuses is a CrowdSec alert of kind `waf` naming the rule, its description, the path and the public address (ASSUMPTIONS P10); Roostack reads them with `cscli alerts list --kind waf` (kept a minute, for pages that poll). The service's page, while it's public, has a **Known exploits** card: how many were refused this week, and the latest three with what they tried ("PHPUnit RCE (CVE-2017-9841)"), the path without its query, when, the visitor's address and the rule; the weekly summary adds "refused 7 requests that tried a known exploit (Vaultwarden 5, Immich 2)". Never the body, headers or query string, which can carry passwords and tokens; the path and address are shown because they're what tells an Owner a refused request was their own. The weekly attack count leaves these alerts out (they're counted as refused requests; a ban that follows is an attack).
       - **False positives:** an Owner notices a part of a public service failing from outside with the refused page (*That one ruffled some feathers*, §6.3.4; a bare "403 Forbidden" until 2026-10-19) but working at home; the card shows the refused request (their own address, the path, the rule). They turn the blocking off for that service and report the rule to CrowdSec's Hub; the help (Make a service public → If it refuses something it shouldn't) says so, and how a technical user ends a ban.
     - **Later: generic rules (the OWASP Core Rule Set), per service:** broad protection (SQL injection, cross-site scripting and so on) but notorious false positives on modern apps (large uploads, JSON APIs, sync clients). Run in "detect only" first, and each catalog package ships the exclusions it needs (e.g. no body inspection on its upload path), tested with the package. That per-package knowledge (logins, uploads, exclusions) is Roostack's contribution, alongside the login paths it already rate-limits.
     - **Considered:** Coraza (the OWASP engine, with a Caddy module) running the Core Rule Set directly: a possible engine for the later step, but a new Caddy plugin (a dependency to agree). ModSecurity, BunkerWeb, open-appsec and SafeLine are built around other proxies (nginx, Apache, their own), a poor fit for Caddy.
5. **Baseline:** automatic security updates (§3.2), Caddy rate limiting on login pages, secrets in files readable only by Roostack (0600), and container images only from trusted registries, pinned to specific versions.
6. **Wrong two-factor codes — as built (2026-09-29):** limited per account, not per login, since someone with the password could otherwise log in again for more tries. Every fifth wrong code in a row locks the account's codes (even the right one) for 15 minutes, doubling each time up to a day; the right code resets the count. That leaves a guesser about five tries a day, while someone mistyping waits 15 minutes. The count is in the database (a restart doesn't clear it), covers the login and the recovery key page (§6.9), and each lock is audited; the password step says so when locked, telling people that wrong codes they didn't enter mean their password is known. Checks run one at a time, so parallel guesses can't slip past. `roostack user reset-2fa` ends a lock. Considered: a limit per login (what we had; it limited nothing), locking the whole account (the lock is only on codes, so it can't be used against an account whose password the attacker doesn't have), and relying on Caddy's rate limiting alone (per address, so it doesn't stop guesses from many addresses).

### 6.11 Public access — Locked (2026-09-30), details Open

Nothing is public unless the Owner makes it so (§1 principle 6). The Owner makes one of a service's addresses public from the service's page, or, on a lab on the internet, when installing it (§6.12); the Roostack UI itself stays private (§6.10 item 2).
- **Who — Locked (2026-10-16):** only an Owner, everywhere: from the service's page, when installing, and the CrowdSec sharing question (a lab-wide setting). An Admin installing on a lab on the internet sees no public choice and no sharing question; the service starts private, and the form says an Owner can make it public from its page. Each install's audit entry records the service, the server it runs on, whether it went public, and any risks the Owner accepted for a package of their own (§8.3.4). Found by the security review of 2026-10-16 (M15): the install form let an Admin make a service public and answer the sharing question, which this section and §6.12 disagreed about. Considered: letting Admins choose at install (Admins manage services, but exposing one to the internet is a host-level risk, like the rest of public access).

- **Ways in ("entries"), all to be supported:**
  1. **Port forwarding at home:** the router forwards TCP 80 and 443 (and UDP 443) to the server; public records point at the home's public address. Built first: it needs nothing beyond the home router.
  2. **Through a cloud server in the lab** (an "edge", §10.5): no router change, and the home's address stays hidden.
  3. **Through Roostack Cloud** (later, §4): the same edge mechanism, run by Roostack Cloud, for people without a cloud server of their own or behind CGNAT.

  A service that runs on a cloud server is reached at that server directly. The entry is a seam in the code from the start (where public records point, and how traffic reaches the service's server), so 2 and 3 slot in without reworking 1.
